```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	Unless otherwise noted, all experiments in this section use the v1 prompt (\S\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).
5	
6	\subsection{Count-Degradation Curves}
7	\label{sec:count_curves}
8	
9	\begin{figure}[t]
10	\centering
11	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\columnwidth}
12	\centering
13	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_count_curves.pdf}
14	\end{minipage}
15	\hfill
16	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\columnwidth}
17	\centering
18	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_bcs_scaling.pdf}
19	\end{minipage}
20	\caption{\textbf{(a)} Count-degradation curves for NER and RE. Error bars show $\pm 1$\,SD across 3 seeds (42, 123, 456) $\times$ 25 batches per seed. RE accuracy declines monotonically with count; NER recall exhibits a U-shaped pattern with minimum at intermediate counts. Full-schema points (rightmost, hollow markers) represent the complete type inventory with no batch sampling variance. \textbf{(b)} BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. The BCS--accuracy slope is consistent across the independent 16-relation pool and within-pool 16-relation subsets (permutation $p = 0.60$); the wider BCS range available at 80 relations produces larger absolute gaps.}
21	\label{fig:main_results}
22	\end{figure}
23	
24	Figure~\ref{fig:main_results}a shows RE accuracy declines monotonically with schema size (0.86$\to$0.50 for FewRel). NER recall declines through intermediate counts (0.51$\to$0.27, plateauing across counts 22--44) before partial recovery at full-schema (0.41), where the elimination of type-filtering overhead restores extraction performance. DocRED replicates the monotonic RE pattern (88.0\%$\to$38.7\%, $-$56\%; Appendix). MAVEN exhibits a reversed trend attributable to centrality confounding (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). The effect replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($-$43\,pp on Few-NERD; per-batch $r = 0.725$; Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}), indicating schema configuration rather than model-specific factors predicts difficulty. The pattern replicates across three seeds (42, 123, 456; max $\Delta = 10.7$\,pp, within 1\,SD); intra-batch variance exceeds inter-seed variance by ${>}$5$\times$ at every count level (Appendix~\ref{app:variance-decomposition}). Formal trend analysis confirms monotonic RE decline (Jonckheere-Terpstra $J = 109{,}707$, $p < 0.001$, pooled across three seeds). Count curves (25 batches per level) establish trend direction with error bars; magnitude estimation relies on the higher-powered BCS-controlled experiments (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).
25	
26	\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}
27	\label{sec:pairwise}
28	
29	We test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate (the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$) and correlate it with $\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\% CIs.
30	
31	\begin{table}[t]
32	\centering
33	\small
34	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
35	\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}
36	\toprule
37	\textbf{Dataset} & \textbf{Embed.} & \textbf{$n$ pairs} & \textbf{$r$} & \textbf{$r^2$} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{$p$} \\
38	\midrule
39	Few-NERD & Qwen3 & 1{,}953 & 0.112 & 0.013 & {[}0.076, 0.156{]} & ${<}\,0.001$ \\
40	MAVEN & MiniLM & 11{,}026 & 0.129 & 0.017 & {[}0.104, 0.156{]} & ${<}\,0.001$ \\
41	\bottomrule
42	\end{tabular}}
43	\caption{Pairwise SCS--confusion correlation. $r^2$ indicates the fraction of variance in type confusion explained by embedding similarity. Both correlations are modest in magnitude but robust across datasets and embedding models (both $p \ll 0.001$). Confusion rate for each ordered pair $(t_i, t_j)$ is computed as the total bidirectional confusion count $C(t_i{\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\to}t_i)$ divided by the sum of gold frequencies, aggregated over all count conditions; CIs are pair-level percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). FewRel ($r = 0.187$; \S\ref{sec:scs-validation}) is excluded as RE's per-instance format precludes direct comparison of confusion rates. Type-level cluster bootstrap CIs: Few-NERD [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN [0.081, 0.185]; both exclude zero, confirming robustness to within-type correlation.}
44	\label{tab:pairwise}
45	\end{table}
46	
47	The correlations are statistically significant but modest: $r^2 \approx 1.3\text{--}1.7\%$. Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). Both datasets show significant positive correlations between pairwise SCS and type confusion rates. Spearman correlations corroborate: $\rho = 0.144\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\rho = 0.27\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).
48	
49	The remaining variance likely reflects entity frequency, prompt position \cite{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. Although pairwise $r^2$ is modest, the signal compounds at the batch level into quartile gaps of 3.9--5.3\,pp under typical batching (\S\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}). Notably, the independent MiniLM embedding yields a \emph{stronger} SCS--confusion correlation on Few-NERD ($r = 0.172$ vs.\ Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix), suggesting that shared-representation bias is conservative rather than inflationary.
50	
51	\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}
52	\label{sec:bcs_controlled}
53	
54	We fix type count and vary BCS. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\S\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.
55	
56	In BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\Delta=-5.3$\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison; full data in Appendix~\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\,=\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.
57	
58	\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}
59	The effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\rho = -0.661$, $p < 0.001$) and $-$17.6\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.
60	
61	\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}
62	\label{sec:within_pool}
63	Within-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \approx 0.60$; Appendix~\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.
64	
65	\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}
66	\label{sec:practical_magnitude}
67	Direct regression on 100 random 16-type batches from the 80-relation pool shows BCS explains 22\% of cross-batch accuracy variance ($R^2 = 0.218$, $p < 0.001$), scaling to 53\% at DocRED 96-rel. The typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\,pp (95\% CI: [0.4, 4.0]), roughly 7\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\% vs.\ 78.9\% for top-quartile ($\Delta = 3.9$\,pp). High mean-BCS batches contain more active confusable pairs, concentrating degradation. A post-hoc Monte Carlo simulation reproducing the observed ${\sim}3\times$ pair-to-batch amplification (conditional $R^2 \approx 7\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs to batch $R^2 \approx 22\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation-sim}) confirms this aggregation pattern.
68	
69	\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}
70	The effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\approx$51\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime, suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Dimension~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\S\ref{sec:count_curves}); Dimension~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).
71	
72	\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}
73	Under the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\% vs.\ high-BCS 59.7\%; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.001$), modestly attenuated from v1's extreme-batch gap but highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates magnitude but does not eliminate the effect (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). The BCS--accuracy correlation holds across four structurally distinct prompt variants at count 32 (all $r \leq -0.47$; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}), confirming that the confusability effect is not an artifact of prompt design.
74	
75	\subsection{Multi-Dimensional Degradation}
76	\label{sec:multi_channel}
77	
78	\begin{figure}[t]
79	\centering
80	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}
81	\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\%) and non-monotonic.}
82	\label{fig:error_taxonomy}
83	\end{figure}
84	
85	Error taxonomy decomposition (Table~\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\text{--}27.5\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.
86	
87	These results establish two dimensions: \textbf{Dimension~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \textbf{Dimension~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), consistent with bounded association in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact. The full-range regression ($n = 69$, five count levels) serves as the primary separability test; the $2 \times 2$ factorial ($n = 54$) as a sensitivity check. TOST equivalence testing confirms bounded association (interaction = 0.47\,pp, $p_{\text{TOST}} = 0.003$ at $\pm 3$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}).
88	
89	\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}
90	\label{sec:centrality_confound}
91	
92	Per-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality: high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.
```

Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). Both datasets show significant positive correlations between pairwise SCS and type confusion rates. Spearman correlations corroborate: $\\rho = 0.144\\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\\rho = 0.27\\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).\n\nThe remaining variance likely reflects entity frequency, prompt position \\cite{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. Although pairwise $r^2$ is modest, the signal compounds at the batch level into quartile gaps of 3.9--5.3\\,pp under typical batching (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}). Notably, the independent MiniLM embedding yields a \\emph{stronger} SCS--confusion correlation on Few-NERD ($r = 0.172$ vs.\\ Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix), suggesting that shared-representation bias is conservative rather than inflationary.\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}\n\\label{sec:bcs_controlled}\n\nWe fix type count and vary BCS. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\\rho = -0.661$, $p < 0.001$) and $-$17.6\\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nWithin-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nDirect regression on 100 random 16-type batches from the 80-relation pool shows BCS explains 22\\% of cross-batch accuracy variance ($R^2 = 0.218$, $p < 0.001$), scaling to 53\\% at DocRED 96-rel. The typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI: [0.4, 4.0]), roughly 7\\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). High mean-BCS batches contain more active confusable pairs, concentrating degradation. A post-hoc Monte Carlo simulation reproducing the observed ${\\sim}3\\times$ pair-to-batch amplification (conditional $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs to batch $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim}) confirms this aggregation pattern.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime, suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Dimension~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}); Dimension~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.001$), modestly attenuated from v1's extreme-batch gap but highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates magnitude but does not eliminate the effect (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). The BCS--accuracy correlation holds across four structurally distinct prompt variants at count 32 (all $r \\leq -0.47$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}), confirming that the confusability effect is not an artifact of prompt design.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Dimensional Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two dimensions: \\textbf{Dimension~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Dimension~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), consistent with bounded association in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact. The full-range regression ($n = 69$, five count levels) serves as the primary separability test; the $2 \\times 2$ factorial ($n = 54$) as a sensitivity check. TOST equivalence testing confirms bounded association (interaction = 0.47\\,pp, $p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}).\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality: high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.",

85	\section{SDGD Full Protocol and Results}
86	\label{sec:appendix_sdgd}
87	
88	We construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\textsc{largest\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent (i.e., highly confusable) types share a color. Each color class forms one extraction batch, and predictions are merged via set union. We compare four modes: (i)~\textbf{single\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\textbf{sdgd\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\textbf{random\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\textbf{per\_type}, the extreme case where each type is extracted in isolation.
89	
90	Single-pass extraction achieves F1\,=\,0.445 (P\,=\,0.563, R\,=\,0.368). SDGD obtains F1\,=\,0.251 (P\,=\,0.210, R\,=\,0.314), a 44\% relative drop. Random batching reaches F1\,=\,0.320 (P\,=\,0.249, R\,=\,0.446), and per-type extraction yields F1\,=\,0.292 (P\,=\,0.194, R\,=\,0.586). Every decomposition mode falls below the single-pass baseline (Table~\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).
91	
92	\begin{table}[h]
93	\centering
94	\small
95	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
96	\begin{tabular}{lrccc}
97	\toprule
98	\textbf{Mode} & \textbf{\# Batches} & \textbf{Precision} & \textbf{Recall} & \textbf{F1} \\
99	\midrule
100	single\_pass & 1 & 0.563 & 0.368 & 0.445 \\
101	\midrule
102	sdgd\_zero (v1) & 8 & 0.210 & 0.314 & 0.251 \\
103	random\_batch (v1) & 8 & 0.249 & 0.446 & 0.320 \\
104	per\_type & 66 & 0.194 & 0.586 & 0.292 \\
105	\midrule
106	sdgd\_zero (v2) & 4 & 0.324 & 0.191 & 0.241 \\
107	random\_batch (v2) & 8 & 0.331 & 0.276 & 0.301 \\
108	\bottomrule
109	\end{tabular}}
110	\caption{Few-NERD extraction under decomposition modes. V1 uses union merge; v2 uses confidence-gated merge with \texttt{min\_batch\_size=8} (reducing 8$\to$4 batches for sdgd\_zero). All decomposition modes produce lower F1 than the single-pass baseline. All modes use the v1 prompt.}
111	\label{tab:sdgd_results}
112	\end{table}
113	
114	Decomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions, inflating false positives.
115	
116	Our v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\_zero: 0.210$\to$0.324; random\_batch: 0.249$\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\to$0.191; 0.446$\to$0.276), yielding lower F1 in both modes (sdgd\_zero: 0.251$\to$0.241; random\_batch: 0.320$\to$0.301).
117	
118	\subsection{Practical Routing Strategies on RE}

120	
121	We evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition (same grouping as Table~\ref{tab:batch_composition}). Each strategy selects a single sub-schema for each test instance and returns that sub-schema's prediction.

The failure stems from constrained decoding (enum constraint), which forces confident predictions in every sub-schema: 73.4\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, with per-sub-schema none-rates of only 20--40\%.}

144	
145	\section{Factorial Analysis and Separability Test}
146	\label{sec:appendix_factorial}
147	
148	To formally test whether count and confusability effects are additive, we fit a multiple regression (accuracy $\sim$ $\log_2$\,count $+$ BCS $+$ count$\times$BCS) across 69 batches from FewRel spanning five count levels ($k \in \{4, 8, 16, 32, 40\}$). Both main effects are significant (count: $p = 0.008$; BCS: $p = 0.0002$; $R^2 = 0.52$), but the interaction term is negligible (0.47\,pp). A TOST equivalence test with a $\pm 3$\,pp margin confirms that the interaction is bounded well below practical significance ($p_{\text{TOST}} = 0.003$), supporting bounded association between count and confusability effects.
149	
150	A $2 \times 2$ factorial sensitivity check on FewRel (count $\in \{8, 16\}$, BCS low/high; $n = 54$) was inconclusive ($p_{\text{TOST}} = 0.56$) due to limited power; this does not contradict the primary finding but limits generalization beyond the regression framework. The Few-NERD factorial (\S\ref{sec:multi_channel}) similarly did not reach significance ($p = 0.50$). The regression approach provides the stronger test by leveraging the full count range.
151	
152	\paragraph{Equivalence margin justification.}
153	We set the $\pm 3$\,pp margin based on three converging anchors. \emph{Empirical}: typical random-batch confusability variation produces 2--5\,pp accuracy differences (batch SD $\approx 4.6$\,pp at count 32). \emph{Practical}: a ${<}3$\,pp accuracy difference falls below deployment-relevant thresholds. \emph{Statistical}: 3\,pp $\approx 0.5 \times$ batch SD, equivalent to Cohen's $d \approx 0.5$ (medium effect threshold; \citealt{lakens-2017-equivalence}). A 3\,pp interaction atop the 2.2\,pp confusability main effect would represent ${>}130\%$ amplification, fundamentally changing whether confusability warrants mitigation. At $\pm 2$\,pp (marginally equivalent, $p = 0.047$), interaction remains bounded below the main effect itself.
154	
155	\begin{table}[h]
156	\centering
157	\small
158	\begin{tabular}{crrl}
159	\toprule
160	\textbf{Margin (pp)} & \textbf{$t_{\text{upper}}$} & \textbf{$p_{\text{TOST}}$} & \textbf{Conclusion} \\
161	\midrule
162	$\pm 1$ & $-0.59$ & $0.278$ & Inconclusive \\
163	$\pm 2$ & $-1.70$ & $0.047$ & Equivalent \\
164	$\pm 3$ & $-2.81$ & $0.003$ & Equivalent \\
165	$\pm 5$ & $-5.02$ & ${<}\,0.001$ & Equivalent \\
166	\bottomrule
167	\end{tabular}
168	\caption{TOST sensitivity analysis across equivalence margins. The interaction effect (0.47\,pp, 90\% CI [$-$1.0, 2.0]\,pp) is bounded below 2\,pp at marginal significance ($p = 0.047$), confirming that the $\pm 3$\,pp choice does not drive the equivalence conclusion. At $\pm 1$\,pp, equivalence is inconclusive ($p = 0.278$), meaning interaction could reach approximately half the main effect magnitude.}
169	\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}
170	\end{table}
171	
172	\section{Within-Pool CI Details}
173	\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}
174	
175	The quartile $\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\,pp, 95\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (CI: [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.
176	
177	\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}
178	\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}
179	
180	Section~\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.
181	
182	\paragraph{Confound identification.}
183	For each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:
184	\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]
185	\item \textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, producing the positive BCS--F1 association.
186	\item \textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors, central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.
187	\end{itemize}
188	
189	\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}
190	Table~\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.
191	
192	\begin{table}[h]
193	\centering
194	\small
195	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
196	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
197	\toprule
198	\textbf{Embedding} & \textbf{Control} & \textbf{Raw $\rho$} & \textbf{Raw $p$} & \textbf{Partial $r$} & \textbf{Partial $p$} \\
199	\midrule
200	MiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & $0.011$ & --- & --- \\
201	MiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & $0.11$ \\
202	MiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.23$ \\
203	\midrule
204	Qwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & $0.025$ & --- & --- \\
205	Qwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & $0.44$ \\
206	Qwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.44$ \\
207	\bottomrule
208	\end{tabular}}
209	\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \texttt{exp\_maven\_residualized\_bcs}.}
210	\label{tab:residualized_bcs}
211	\end{table}
212	
213	\paragraph{Interpretation.}
214	The two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality (the count of strong semantic neighbors) becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.
215	
216	Under joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$, confirming that the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.
217	
218	\section{Batch Composition Results}
219	\label{sec:appendix_batch_composition}
220	
221	See Table~\ref{tab:batch_composition} and the accompanying discussion in \S\ref{sec:sdgd}.
222	
223	\input{prompt_appendix}
224	
225	\section{Prompt Version Sensitivity Analysis}
226	\label{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}
227	
228	We assess whether the confusability effects reported in \S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}--\ref{sec:multi_channel} are robust to prompt design by replicating key experiments under the v2 prompt (\S\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).
229	
230	\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}
231	Under the v1 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap (Table~\ref{tab:bcs_controlled}) is substantial. Under v2, with $n{=}25$ batches per group and wider BCS contrast (low-BCS range [0.356, 0.367]; high-BCS range [0.791, 0.811]), the gap is $-$25.5\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\% vs.\ high-BCS 59.7\%; 95\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $p < 0.001$; Pearson $r = -0.919$). The effect is significant, directionally consistent with v1, and only modestly attenuated in magnitude ($-$5.7\,pp reduction). An earlier $n{=}5$ evaluation with narrower BCS contrast (0.21) yielded a smaller gap of $-$7.9\,pp (CI: [$-$5.2, $+$21.5]), illustrating how limited statistical power and restricted BCS range can obscure the underlying signal, a cautionary note for small-sample prompt sensitivity studies.
232	
233	\paragraph{NER (Few-NERD).}
234	The batch-level BCS--F1 correlation under v2 is consistent with v1: at count${}\,=\,12$, $r = +0.58$ ($p = 0.002$, 95\% CI: [0.19, 0.81]) vs.\ v1 $r = +0.50$ ($p = 0.008$). As discussed in \S\ref{sec:pairwise}, this positive direction reflects a type-frequency confound at the batch level (high-BCS batches contain high-frequency entity types that models extract more reliably) rather than evidence against the confusability hypothesis. The pairwise SCS--confusion analysis (\S\ref{sec:pairwise}), which is immune to this aggregation artifact, remains the primary evidence for the NER confusability dimension.
235	
236	\paragraph{Interpretation.}
237	The $n{=}25$ v2 result confirms that the confusability effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude: the gap narrows ($-$5.7\,pp) but remains highly significant ($p < 0.001$, $r = -0.919$). The earlier $n{=}5$ non-significance reflected insufficient power and narrow BCS contrast, not absence of the effect. V2's stricter format constraints raise the baseline accuracy (low-BCS 85.2\% vs.\ 82.3\%), but type confusion under high confusability persists (59.7\% vs.\ 51.0\%). Prompt design modulates the practical magnitude of the confusability effect but does not eliminate it, ruling out prompt-specific confounding as the primary source. The underlying confusability signal (pairwise SCS--confusion correlation and the directional BCS--accuracy relationship) persists across both prompt versions.
238	
239	\subsection{Cross-Prompt-Variant BCS Correlation}
240	\label{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}
241	
242	To verify that the BCS--accuracy relationship is not an artifact of a specific prompt template, we evaluate four structurally distinct prompt variants on FewRel 80-relation RE at two count levels (8 and 32), with 10 random batches per condition.
243	
244	\begin{table}[h]
245	\centering
246	\small
247	\begin{tabular}{llrrrr}
248	\toprule
249	\textbf{Variant} & \textbf{Count} & \textbf{Mean Acc} & \textbf{$r$} & \textbf{$p$} \\
250	\midrule
251	V2 (definition-first) & 8 & 0.769 & $-$0.333 & 0.348 \\
252	V2 (definition-first) & 32 & 0.564 & $-$0.648 & 0.043 \\
253	Instruction-rewrite & 8 & 0.847 & $-$0.223 & 0.536 \\
254	Instruction-rewrite & 32 & 0.662 & $-$0.469 & 0.171 \\
255	Chain-of-thought & 8 & 0.749 & $+$0.254 & 0.478 \\
256	Chain-of-thought & 32 & 0.617 & $-$0.566 & 0.088 \\
257	Minimal & 8 & 0.852 & $-$0.170 & 0.638 \\
258	Minimal & 32 & 0.615 & $-$0.771 & 0.009 \\
259	\bottomrule
260	\end{tabular}
261	\caption{BCS--accuracy Pearson correlation across four prompt variants. At count 32, all four variants show negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r \leq -0.47$), with two reaching $p < 0.05$. At count 8, the narrower BCS range reduces statistical power.}
262	\label{tab:prompt_variants}
263	\end{table}
264	
265	At count 32, all four variants produce negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r$ ranges from $-$0.47 to $-$0.77), with V2 ($p = 0.043$) and Minimal ($p = 0.009$) reaching significance and Chain-of-thought approaching it ($p = 0.088$). At count 8, correlations are weaker and nonsignificant across all variants, consistent with the narrower BCS range at lower schema complexity reducing the detectable effect size. These results confirm that the confusability effect arises from schema composition, not prompt-specific phrasing.
266	
267	\section{BCS Aggregation Sensitivity}
268	\label{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}
269	
270	We define Batch Confusability Score (BCS) as the \emph{mean} pairwise SCS across all type pairs in a batch (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). An alternative definition takes the \emph{maximum} pairwise SCS within each batch, capturing the single most confusable pair. Here we compare both aggregation methods to verify that core conclusions are robust to this choice.
271	
272	\begin{table}[h]
273	\centering
274	\small
275	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
276	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
277	\toprule
278	\textbf{Aggregation} & \textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Slope} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{$r$} & \textbf{$p$} \\
279	\midrule
280	MEAN (primary) & 16-rel & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\,0.001$ \\
281	MEAN & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\
282	\midrule
283	MAX & 16-rel & $-$0.449 & [$-$0.552, $-$0.346] & $-$0.637 & ${<}\,0.001$ \\
284	MAX & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\
285	\bottomrule
286	\end{tabular}}
287	\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p \approx 0.60$; MAX $p \approx 0.44$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}
288	\label{tab:bcs_aggregation}
289	\end{table}
290	
291	MAX aggregation yields stronger correlations ($r = -0.637$ vs.\ $-0.467$ for 16-rel), consistent with the hypothesis that the single most confusable pair in a batch predicts substitution errors. Both definitions produce significant negative slopes across all conditions, and the permutation tests confirm no significant difference between independent and sampled pools under either definition. We adopt MEAN as the primary definition because it better represents average pairwise difficulty across all schema combinations, while MAX may overweight a single outlier pair. The choice does not affect any paper conclusion.
292

313	\section{Within-Pool Analysis Methodology}
314	\label{app:within-pool-method}
315	
316	A potential confound in the cross-scale comparison is that the 16-relation and 80-relation pools contain different types, so the observed gap could reflect pool composition rather than confusability. To control for this, we draw 100 random 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool and evaluate under the same BCS-controlled protocol.
317

319	\centering
320	\small
321	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%

327	\midrule
328	Independent 16-rel ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\,0.001$ & $-$5.31 & [$-$7.44, $-$3.19] \\
329	80-pool $\to$ 16-sub ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & $0.027$ & $-$3.92 & [$-$8.17, $+$0.38] \\
330	Full 80-rel ($n{=}25$)$^\dagger$ & $+$0.221 & [$-$1.303, $+$1.695] & $+$0.064 & $0.76$ & $-$1.04 & [$-$10.12, $+$8.04] \\

337	\label{tab:within_pool}
338	\end{table}
339	
340	The within-pool slope ($-$0.401, CI excludes zero) is statistically indistinguishable from the independent 16-relation pool ($-$0.509; permutation $p \approx 0.60$). The smaller quartile gap ($-$3.92\,pp vs.\ $-$5.31\,pp) reflects the narrower BCS range within the 80-relation pool (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool}).
341	
342	In the full 80-relation condition, natural BCS variation is insufficient (IQR${}=0.033$) to yield a detectable effect ($n=25$, slope${}=+0.221$, $p \approx 0.76$). This does not invalidate the confusability effect; the within-pool slope is significant ($p=0.027$) when BCS is deliberately varied, but establishes that natural schema sampling at this scale does not produce sufficient BCS contrast for detection.

366	
367	\section{Pair-to-Batch Aggregation Simulation}
368	\label{app:aggregation-sim}
369	
370	The following simulation provides an illustrative explanatory model for how pair-level effects aggregate to batch-level variance. It is not the evidence base for our claims; the observed batch-level $R^2 = 0.218$ (\S\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}) is a direct empirical measurement independent of this simulation.
371	
372	\paragraph{Setup.} We examine whether the pair-level $R^2 \approx 7\%$ (computed over non-zero confusion pairs) can aggregate to the observed batch-level $R^2 \approx 22\%$ via a Monte Carlo simulation. From the FewRel 16-relation experiment, we estimate the inter-pair SCS correlation for pairs sharing a common type ($\rho_\text{SCS} = 0.35$). For batches of $k=8$ types ($\binom{8}{2}=28$ pairs), we simulate 10{,}000 iterations: (1) generate 28 correlated pair-level effects with individual $R^2 = 0.074$; (2) average to obtain batch-level signals; (3) compute batch-level $R^2$.
373	
374	\paragraph{Results.} Simulated batch $R^2 = 0.219$ [95\% CI: 0.092--0.361], matching the observed value of 0.218. The effective amplification factor is $\sim$3$\times$, explained by signal correlation within batches preserving variance while independent noise averages out (effective degrees of freedom $\approx 3.4$, not 28).
375	
376	\paragraph{Unconditional baseline.}
377	When the simulation uses the unconditional pairwise $r^2 = 0.013$ (computed over all type pairs including zero-confusion pairs; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}), the simulated batch-level $R^2$ drops to $0.044$ [95\% CI: 0.001--0.151], well below the observed $R^2 = 0.218$. The analytical formula confirms: $\text{SNR} \times \text{EF} / (\text{SNR} \times \text{EF} + 1) = 0.042$ with the same effective factor $\text{EF} = 3.35$. This ${\sim}5\times$ gap reflects the dilution from zero-confusion pairs: approximately 89\% of type pairs never exhibit mutual confusion, contributing only noise to the unconditional correlation. The conditional model ($R^2 = 0.074 \to 0.219$) matches the observed value because it isolates the minority of semantically proximate pairs that actually compete during type resolution; BCS predicts batch accuracy through the density of \emph{active} confusable pairs, not uniform contributions from all pairs.
378	
379	\section{Split-Origin Covariate Analysis}
380	\label{app:split-origin}
381	
382	FewRel combines 64 train-split and 16 val-split relation types into a single 80-type pool.  Train-origin types show numerically lower accuracy ($-8.8$\,pp, $p = 0.06$; see Limitations), raising the question of whether differential pre-training exposure confounds the BCS--accuracy relationship.  We test this by adding a per-batch \emph{train-origin ratio} (fraction of relation types drawn from the train split) as a covariate in the BCS--accuracy regression.
383	
384	\paragraph{Method.}  Across 213 multi-type batches ($k \geq 2$) from the batch-composition experiment (\S\ref{sec:sdgd}) and the factorial design (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}), we compute $\texttt{train\_origin\_ratio} = n_{\text{train}} / k$ for each batch.  The ratio ranges from 0.40 to 1.00 (mean 0.80, reflecting the 64/80 base rate).  We fit:
385	\[
386	\text{accuracy} = \beta_0 + \beta_{\text{BCS}} \cdot \text{BCS} + \beta_{\text{origin}} \cdot \text{train\_origin\_ratio} + \varepsilon
387	\]
388	
389	\paragraph{Results.}  The BCS partial effect is unchanged after controlling for split-origin: $\beta_{\text{BCS}} = -0.416$, $p < 10^{-16}$ (vs.\ $\beta = -0.412$, $p < 10^{-16}$ without the covariate).  Train-origin ratio is not independently significant ($\beta_{\text{origin}} = -0.057$, $p = 0.245$), and $\Delta R^2 = 0.005$.  The BCS--accuracy relationship is therefore not confounded by differential pre-training exposure at the batch level.
390	
391	Pairwise SCS distributions do not differ significantly between train-origin (64 types, mean\,=\,0.880) and val-origin (16 types, mean\,=\,0.874) subsets (Mann-Whitney $p = 0.754$, Cohen's $d = 0.081$), confirming that the train/val split does not introduce systematic confusability bias.
392	
393	\section{Cross-Domain Connections}
394	\label{app:cross-domain}
395	
396	The schema-induced degradation documented here is not unique to information extraction. Schema-Guided Dialogue systems (SGD/SGD-X; \citealt{rastogi2020towards}) face an analogous challenge: as the number of intent and slot types grows, dialogue state tracking accuracy declines. \citet{li2023evaluating} observed count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT-based IE, attributing it to prompt overload. Our two-dimensional decomposition offers a more refined explanation: schema size per se is not the sole predictor; rather, larger schemas increase the probability of including high-SCS pairs, which contribute disproportionately to confusion. This distinction has practical implications: a schema of 80 types with low mutual confusability may outperform a 40-type schema with clustered semantics, and suggests that confusability-aware design principles may generalize to any task where an LLM must discriminate among a prompt-specified label inventory.
397	
398	\section{Additional Experimental Details}
399	\label{sec:appendix_additional_details}
400	
401	\subsection{Cross-Seed Variance Decomposition}
402	\label{app:variance-decomposition}
403	
404	To quantify whether performance variability is attributable to random batch composition or seed-dependent model inference noise, we decompose total variance into intra-seed (batch) and inter-seed components across three seeds (42, 123, 456), each evaluated on 25 random batches per count level.

415	16 & 0.0626 & 0.0270 & 5.4 \\
416	32 & 0.0462 & 0.0091 & 26.0 \\
417	40 & 0.0421 & 0.0019 & 474.4 \\
418	64 & 0.0245 & 0.0022 & 119.8 \\
419	80 & 0.0001 & 0.0088 & $<$0.1 \\
420	\bottomrule
421	\end{tabular}
422	\caption{Variance decomposition across count levels on FewRel. Batch SD is the root-mean intra-seed variance across 25 random type batches (averaged over 3 seeds). Seed SD is the standard deviation of the 3 per-seed means. Ratio = mean batch variance / seed variance. At count 80, only one batch composition exists (the full schema), so batch variance vanishes by construction.}
423	\label{tab:variance_decomposition}
424	\end{table}
425	
426	\begin{table}[h]
427	\centering
428	\small
429	\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
430	\toprule
431	\textbf{Count} & \textbf{Batch SD} & \textbf{Seed SD} & \textbf{Ratio} \\
432	\midrule
433	2 & 0.1782 & 0.0557 & 10.2 \\
434	6 & 0.1233 & 0.0160 & 59.2 \\
435	12 & 0.1063 & 0.0297 & 12.8 \\
436	22 & 0.0954 & 0.0096 & 99.2 \\

441	\caption{Variance decomposition on Few-NERD (3 seeds $\times$ 25 batches per count level). Batch SD: root-mean intra-seed standard deviation averaged over seeds. Seed SD: standard deviation of per-seed means. Ratio = batch variance / seed variance. At count 66 (full schema), only one composition exists, so both components collapse to near-zero.}
442	\label{tab:variance_decomposition_ner}
443	\end{table}
444	
445	Intra-batch variance dominates inter-seed variance by 5--474$\times$ on FewRel (mean 125$\times$, counts 4--64) and 10--99$\times$ on Few-NERD (mean 51$\times$, counts 2--44), confirming that random batch composition is the primary source of performance variability on both tasks. Under greedy decoding (temperature${}\,=\,0$), model inference is deterministic; the inter-seed variance reflects differences in which random batch compositions each seed generates, not inference stochasticity. The wide ratio range reflects variation in how tightly seed means cluster at each count level; even the minimum ratio (5.4$\times$ on FewRel at count 16, 10.2$\times$ on Few-NERD at count 2) indicates batch composition contributes the majority of observed variance. At count 80 (FewRel) and count 66 (Few-NERD), the full schema admits only one batch composition, collapsing batch variance by construction.

NER has 4 BCS bins; RE has 5.}\n\\label{tab:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{SDGD Full Protocol and Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_sdgd}\n\nWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent (i.e., highly confusable) types share a color. Each color class forms one extraction batch, and predictions are merged via set union. We compare four modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, the extreme case where each type is extracted in isolation.\n\nSingle-pass extraction achieves F1\\,=\\,0.445 (P\\,=\\,0.563, R\\,=\\,0.368). SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251 (P\\,=\\,0.210, R\\,=\\,0.314), a 44\\% relative drop. Random batching reaches F1\\,=\\,0.320 (P\\,=\\,0.249, R\\,=\\,0.446), and per-type extraction yields F1\\,=\\,0.292 (P\\,=\\,0.194, R\\,=\\,0.586). Every decomposition mode falls below the single-pass baseline (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Mode} & \\textbf{\\# Batches} & \\textbf{Precision} & \\textbf{Recall} & \\textbf{F1} \\\\\n\\midrule\nsingle\\_pass & 1 & 0.563 & 0.368 & 0.445 \\\\\n\\midrule\nsdgd\\_zero (v1) & 8 & 0.210 & 0.314 & 0.251 \\\\\nrandom\\_batch (v1) & 8 & 0.249 & 0.446 & 0.320 \\\\\nper\\_type & 66 & 0.194 & 0.586 & 0.292 \\\\\n\\midrule\nsdgd\\_zero (v2) & 4 & 0.324 & 0.191 & 0.241 \\\\\nrandom\\_batch (v2) & 8 & 0.331 & 0.276 & 0.301 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Few-NERD extraction under decomposition modes. V1 uses union merge; v2 uses confidence-gated merge with \\texttt{min\\_batch\\_size=8} (reducing 8$\\to$4 batches for sdgd\\_zero). All decomposition modes produce lower F1 than the single-pass baseline. All modes use the v1 prompt.}\n\\label{tab:sdgd_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nDecomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions, inflating false positives.\n\nOur v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\\_zero: 0.210$\\to$0.324; random\\_batch: 0.249$\\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\\to$0.191; 0.446$\\to$0.276), yielding lower F1 in both modes (sdgd\\_zero: 0.251$\\to$0.241; random\\_batch: 0.320$\\to$0.301).\n\n\\subsection{Practical Routing Strategies on RE}\n\\label{sec:appendix_routing}\n\nWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition (same grouping as Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). Each strategy selects a single sub-schema for each test instance and returns that sub-schema's prediction.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Strategy} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ Single-Pass} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle (gold label) & 72.4\\% & +22.0\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nMax-conf (positive only) & 36.5\\% & $-$14.0\\,pp \\\\\nNone-aware & 36.5\\% & $-$14.0\\,pp \\\\\nMargin (positive only) & 35.9\\% & $-$14.5\\,pp \\\\\nMax-conf (all) & 27.7\\% & $-$22.7\\,pp \\\\\nRandom & 14.7\\% & $-$35.7\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (no decomposition) & 50.4\\% & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Routing strategy comparison on FewRel 80-relation RE with graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition. Oracle requires gold labels. All practical (non-oracle) strategies perform below the single-pass baseline. The failure stems from constrained decoding (enum constraint), which forces confident predictions in every sub-schema: 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, with per-sub-schema none-rates of only 20--40\\%.}\n\\label{tab:routing_strategies}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Factorial Analysis and Separability Test}\n\\label{sec:appendix_factorial}\n\nTo formally test whether count and confusability effects are additive, we fit a multiple regression (accuracy $\\sim$ $\\log_2$\\,count $+$ BCS $+$ count$\\times$BCS) across 69 batches from FewRel spanning five count levels ($k \\in \\{4, 8, 16, 32, 40\\}$). Both main effects are significant (count: $p = 0.008$; BCS: $p = 0.0002$; $R^2 = 0.52$), but the interaction term is negligible (0.47\\,pp). A TOST equivalence test with a $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin confirms that the interaction is bounded well below practical significance ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$), supporting bounded association between count and confusability effects.\n\nA $2 \\times 2$ factorial sensitivity check on FewRel (count $\\in \\{8, 16\\}$, BCS low/high; $n = 54$) was inconclusive ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.56$) due to limited power; this does not contradict the primary finding but limits generalization beyond the regression framework. The Few-NERD factorial (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}) similarly did not reach significance ($p = 0.50$). The regression approach provides the stronger test by leveraging the full count range.\n\n\\paragraph{Equivalence margin justification.}\nWe set the $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin based on three converging anchors. \\emph{Empirical}: typical random-batch confusability variation produces 2--5\\,pp accuracy differences (batch SD $\\approx 4.6$\\,pp at count 32). \\emph{Practical}: a ${<}3$\\,pp accuracy difference falls below deployment-relevant thresholds. \\emph{Statistical}: 3\\,pp $\\approx 0.5 \\times$ batch SD, equivalent to Cohen's $d \\approx 0.5$ (medium effect threshold; \\citealt{lakens-2017-equivalence}). A 3\\,pp interaction atop the 2.2\\,pp confusability main effect would represent ${>}130\\%$ amplification, fundamentally changing whether confusability warrants mitigation. At $\\pm 2$\\,pp (marginally equivalent, $p = 0.047$), interaction remains bounded below the main effect itself.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{crrl}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Margin (pp)} & \\textbf{$t_{\\text{upper}}$} & \\textbf{$p_{\\text{TOST}}$} & \\textbf{Conclusion} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\pm 1$ & $-0.59$ & $0.278$ & Inconclusive \\\\\n$\\pm 2$ & $-1.70$ & $0.047$ & Equivalent \\\\\n$\\pm 3$ & $-2.81$ & $0.003$ & Equivalent \\\\\n$\\pm 5$ & $-5.02$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ & Equivalent \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{TOST sensitivity analysis across equivalence margins. The interaction effect (0.47\\,pp, 90\\% CI [$-$1.0, 2.0]\\,pp) is bounded below 2\\,pp at marginal significance ($p = 0.047$), confirming that the $\\pm 3$\\,pp choice does not drive the equivalence conclusion. At $\\pm 1$\\,pp, equivalence is inconclusive ($p = 0.278$), meaning interaction could reach approximately half the main effect magnitude.}\n\\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Within-Pool CI Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}\n\nThe quartile $\\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\\,pp, 95\\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (CI: [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.\n\n\\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \\emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound identification.}\nFor each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:\n\\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, producing the positive BCS--F1 association.\n\\item \\textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors, central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Embedding} & \\textbf{Control} & \\textbf{Raw $\\rho$} & \\textbf{Raw $p$} & \\textbf{Partial $r$} & \\textbf{Partial $p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & $0.011$ & --- & --- \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & $0.11$ \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.23$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & $0.025$ & --- & --- \\\\\nQwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & $0.44$ \\\\\nQwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.44$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \\texttt{exp\\_maven\\_residualized\\_bcs}.}\n\\label{tab:residualized_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality (the count of strong semantic neighbors) becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.\n\nUnder joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$, confirming that the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.\n\n\\section{Batch Composition Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_batch_composition}\n\nSee Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition} and the accompanying discussion in \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}.\n\n\\input{prompt_appendix}\n\n\\section{Prompt Version Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\nWe assess whether the confusability effects reported in \\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}--\\ref{sec:multi_channel} are robust to prompt design by replicating key experiments under the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}\nUnder the v1 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap (Table~\\ref{tab:bcs_controlled}) is substantial. Under v2, with $n{=}25$ batches per group and wider BCS contrast (low-BCS range [0.356, 0.367]; high-BCS range [0.791, 0.811]), the gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; 95\\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $p < 0.001$; Pearson $r = -0.919$). The effect is significant, directionally consistent with v1, and only modestly attenuated in magnitude ($-$5.7\\,pp reduction). An earlier $n{=}5$ evaluation with narrower BCS contrast (0.21) yielded a smaller gap of $-$7.9\\,pp (CI: [$-$5.2, $+$21.5]), illustrating how limited statistical power and restricted BCS range can obscure the underlying signal, a cautionary note for small-sample prompt sensitivity studies.\n\n\\paragraph{NER (Few-NERD).}\nThe batch-level BCS--F1 correlation under v2 is consistent with v1: at count${}\\,=\\,12$, $r = +0.58$ ($p = 0.002$, 95\\% CI: [0.19, 0.81]) vs.\\ v1 $r = +0.50$ ($p = 0.008$). As discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}, this positive direction reflects a type-frequency confound at the batch level (high-BCS batches contain high-frequency entity types that models extract more reliably) rather than evidence against the confusability hypothesis. The pairwise SCS--confusion analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}), which is immune to this aggregation artifact, remains the primary evidence for the NER confusability dimension.\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe $n{=}25$ v2 result confirms that the confusability effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude: the gap narrows ($-$5.7\\,pp) but remains highly significant ($p < 0.001$, $r = -0.919$). The earlier $n{=}5$ non-significance reflected insufficient power and narrow BCS contrast, not absence of the effect. V2's stricter format constraints raise the baseline accuracy (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ 82.3\\%), but type confusion under high confusability persists (59.7\\% vs.\\ 51.0\\%). Prompt design modulates the practical magnitude of the confusability effect but does not eliminate it, ruling out prompt-specific confounding as the primary source. The underlying confusability signal (pairwise SCS--confusion correlation and the directional BCS--accuracy relationship) persists across both prompt versions.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Prompt-Variant BCS Correlation}\n\\label{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}\n\nTo verify that the BCS--accuracy relationship is not an artifact of a specific prompt template, we evaluate four structurally distinct prompt variants on FewRel 80-relation RE at two count levels (8 and 32), with 10 random batches per condition.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Mean Acc} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nV2 (definition-first) & 8 & 0.769 & $-$0.333 & 0.348 \\\\\nV2 (definition-first) & 32 & 0.564 & $-$0.648 & 0.043 \\\\\nInstruction-rewrite & 8 & 0.847 & $-$0.223 & 0.536 \\\\\nInstruction-rewrite & 32 & 0.662 & $-$0.469 & 0.171 \\\\\nChain-of-thought & 8 & 0.749 & $+$0.254 & 0.478 \\\\\nChain-of-thought & 32 & 0.617 & $-$0.566 & 0.088 \\\\\nMinimal & 8 & 0.852 & $-$0.170 & 0.638 \\\\\nMinimal & 32 & 0.615 & $-$0.771 & 0.009 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{BCS--accuracy Pearson correlation across four prompt variants. At count 32, all four variants show negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r \\leq -0.47$), with two reaching $p < 0.05$. At count 8, the narrower BCS range reduces statistical power.}\n\\label{tab:prompt_variants}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt count 32, all four variants produce negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r$ ranges from $-$0.47 to $-$0.77), with V2 ($p = 0.043$) and Minimal ($p = 0.009$) reaching significance and Chain-of-thought approaching it ($p = 0.088$). At count 8, correlations are weaker and nonsignificant across all variants, consistent with the narrower BCS range at lower schema complexity reducing the detectable effect size. These results confirm that the confusability effect arises from schema composition, not prompt-specific phrasing.\n\n\\section{BCS Aggregation Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}\n\nWe define Batch Confusability Score (BCS) as the \\emph{mean} pairwise SCS across all type pairs in a batch (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). An alternative definition takes the \\emph{maximum} pairwise SCS within each batch, capturing the single most confusable pair. Here we compare both aggregation methods to verify that core conclusions are robust to this choice.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Aggregation} & \\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMEAN (primary) & 16-rel & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMEAN & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMAX & 16-rel & $-$0.449 & [$-$0.552, $-$0.346] & $-$0.637 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAX & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p \\approx 0.60$; MAX $p \\approx 0.44$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\nMAX aggregation yields stronger correlations ($r = -0.637$ vs.\\ $-0.467$ for 16-rel), consistent with the hypothesis that the single most confusable pair in a batch predicts substitution errors. Both definitions produce significant negative slopes across all conditions, and the permutation tests confirm no significant difference between independent and sampled pools under either definition. We adopt MEAN as the primary definition because it better represents average pairwise difficulty across all schema combinations, while MAX may overweight a single outlier pair. The choice does not affect any paper conclusion.\n\n\\section{BCS-Controlled Detailed Results}\n\\label{app:bcs-controlled-table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Schema} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Low-BCS} & \\textbf{High-BCS} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\n16-rel & 8 & 88.9\\% & 79.8\\% & $-$9.1 & --- \\\\\n80-rel & 8 & 82.3\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$31.2 & {[}17.7, 42.7{]} \\\\\n80-rel & 16 & 82.8\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$31.2 & {[}22.9, 39.6{]} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. Low-BCS batches contain maximally dissimilar types; high-BCS batches contain maximally similar types. The 80-relation extreme-batch gap reflects the wider BCS range; regression slopes are consistent across pools ($p = 0.60$; Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool}; see \\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude} for typical-batch magnitudes). The 16-relation condition used a single extreme batch per BCS level; bootstrap CIs require multiple batches.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_controlled}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Within-Pool Analysis Methodology}\n\\label{app:within-pool-method}\n\nA potential confound in the cross-scale comparison is that the 16-relation and 80-relation pools contain different types, so the observed gap could reflect pool composition rather than confusability. To control for this, we draw 100 random 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool and evaluate under the same BCS-controlled protocol.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textbf{Regression (BCS $\\to$ Accuracy)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Quartile $\\Delta$}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n\\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIndependent 16-rel ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\\,0.001$ & $-$5.31 & [$-$7.44, $-$3.19] \\\\\n80-pool $\\to$ 16-sub ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & $0.027$ & $-$3.92 & [$-$8.17, $+$0.38] \\\\\nFull 80-rel ($n{=}25$)$^\\dagger$ & $+$0.221 & [$-$1.303, $+$1.695] & $+$0.064 & $0.76$ & $-$1.04 & [$-$10.12, $+$8.04] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize Permutation test (16-rel vs.\\ 80-pool slopes): $p \\approx 0.60$ (5{,}000 permutations)} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$BCS range (IQR\\,=\\,0.033) insufficient for regression; slope not interpretable.} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Three-way BCS--accuracy analysis on FewRel. Slope: OLS of batch accuracy on BCS (bootstrap CIs, $B{=}10{,}000$). $\\Delta$: accuracy gap between top- and bottom-quartile BCS batches. See Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool} for CI interpretation.}\n\\label{tab:within_pool}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe within-pool slope ($-$0.401, CI excludes zero) is statistically indistinguishable from the independent 16-relation pool ($-$0.509; permutation $p \\approx 0.60$). The smaller quartile gap ($-$3.92\\,pp vs.\\ $-$5.31\\,pp) reflects the narrower BCS range within the 80-relation pool (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool}).\n\nIn the full 80-relation condition, natural BCS variation is insufficient (IQR${}=0.033$) to yield a detectable effect ($n=25$, slope${}=+0.221$, $p \\approx 0.76$). This does not invalidate the confusability effect; the within-pool slope is significant ($p=0.027$) when BCS is deliberately varied, but establishes that natural schema sampling at this scale does not produce sufficient BCS contrast for detection.\n\n\\section{SCS Embedding Ablation: Name-Only vs. Name+Definition}\n\\label{app:scs-ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scs-ablation} compares predictive accuracy of name-only SCS against name+definition SCS across three datasets.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nDataset & SCS$_\\text{name}$ $r$ & SCS$_\\text{def}$ $r$ & $\\Delta r$ & Matrix corr. \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & 0.172 & 0.149 & $-$0.023 & 0.894 \\\\\nMAVEN & 0.129 & 0.164 & +0.035 & 0.656 \\\\\nFewRel & 0.178 & 0.193 & +0.015 & 0.848 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Pearson correlation between SCS and empirical confusion rate. ``Def'' concatenates type name with definition before embedding. All correlations significant ($p < 0.001$). The inconsistent direction of $\\Delta r$ indicates no systematic benefit from including definitions.}\n\\label{tab:scs-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nFew-NERD is the only dataset whose prompts include type definitions; for MAVEN and FewRel, prompts use type names alone, making name-only SCS a direct match to model input. The high matrix correlation (0.66--0.89) confirms that type names capture the dominant semantic structure.\n\n\\section{Pair-to-Batch Aggregation Simulation}\n\\label{app:aggregation-sim}\n\nThe following simulation provides an illustrative explanatory model for how pair-level effects aggregate to batch-level variance. It is not the evidence base for our claims; the observed batch-level $R^2 = 0.218$ (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}) is a direct empirical measurement independent of this simulation.\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.} We examine whether the pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ (computed over non-zero confusion pairs) can aggregate to the observed batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ via a Monte Carlo simulation. From the FewRel 16-relation experiment, we estimate the inter-pair SCS correlation for pairs sharing a common type ($\\rho_\\text{SCS} = 0.35$). For batches of $k=8$ types ($\\binom{8}{2}=28$ pairs), we simulate 10{,}000 iterations: (1) generate 28 correlated pair-level effects with individual $R^2 = 0.074$; (2) average to obtain batch-level signals; (3) compute batch-level $R^2$.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.} Simulated batch $R^2 = 0.219$ [95\\% CI: 0.092--0.361], matching the observed value of 0.218. The effective amplification factor is $\\sim$3$\\times$, explained by signal correlation within batches preserving variance while independent noise averages out (effective degrees of freedom $\\approx 3.4$, not 28).\n\n\\paragraph{Unconditional baseline.}\nWhen the simulation uses the unconditional pairwise $r^2 = 0.013$ (computed over all type pairs including zero-confusion pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}), the simulated batch-level $R^2$ drops to $0.044$ [95\\% CI: 0.001--0.151], well below the observed $R^2 = 0.218$. The analytical formula confirms: $\\text{SNR} \\times \\text{EF} / (\\text{SNR} \\times \\text{EF} + 1) = 0.042$ with the same effective factor $\\text{EF} = 3.35$. This ${\\sim}5\\times$ gap reflects the dilution from zero-confusion pairs: approximately 89\\% of type pairs never exhibit mutual confusion, contributing only noise to the unconditional correlation. The conditional model ($R^2 = 0.074 \\to 0.219$) matches the observed value because it isolates the minority of semantically proximate pairs that actually compete during type resolution; BCS predicts batch accuracy through the density of \\emph{active} confusable pairs, not uniform contributions from all pairs.\n\n\\section{Split-Origin Covariate Analysis}\n\\label{app:split-origin}\n\nFewRel combines 64 train-split and 16 val-split relation types into a single 80-type pool.  Train-origin types show numerically lower accuracy ($-8.8$\\,pp, $p = 0.06$; see Limitations), raising the question of whether differential pre-training exposure confounds the BCS--accuracy relationship.  We test this by adding a per-batch \\emph{train-origin ratio} (fraction of relation types drawn from the train split) as a covariate in the BCS--accuracy regression.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}  Across 213 multi-type batches ($k \\geq 2$) from the batch-composition experiment (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}) and the factorial design (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}), we compute $\\texttt{train\\_origin\\_ratio} = n_{\\text{train}} / k$ for each batch.  The ratio ranges from 0.40 to 1.00 (mean 0.80, reflecting the 64/80 base rate).  We fit:\n\\[\n\\text{accuracy} = \\beta_0 + \\beta_{\\text{BCS}} \\cdot \\text{BCS} + \\beta_{\\text{origin}} \\cdot \\text{train\\_origin\\_ratio} + \\varepsilon\n\\]\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}  The BCS partial effect is unchanged after controlling for split-origin: $\\beta_{\\text{BCS}} = -0.416$, $p < 10^{-16}$ (vs.\\ $\\beta = -0.412$, $p < 10^{-16}$ without the covariate).  Train-origin ratio is not independently significant ($\\beta_{\\text{origin}} = -0.057$, $p = 0.245$), and $\\Delta R^2 = 0.005$.  The BCS--accuracy relationship is therefore not confounded by differential pre-training exposure at the batch level.\n\nPairwise SCS distributions do not differ significantly between train-origin (64 types, mean\\,=\\,0.880) and val-origin (16 types, mean\\,=\\,0.874) subsets (Mann-Whitney $p = 0.754$, Cohen's $d = 0.081$), confirming that the train/val split does not introduce systematic confusability bias.\n\n\\section{Cross-Domain Connections}\n\\label{app:cross-domain}\n\nThe schema-induced degradation documented here is not unique to information extraction. Schema-Guided Dialogue systems (SGD/SGD-X; \\citealt{rastogi2020towards}) face an analogous challenge: as the number of intent and slot types grows, dialogue state tracking accuracy declines. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observed count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT-based IE, attributing it to prompt overload. Our two-dimensional decomposition offers a more refined explanation: schema size per se is not the sole predictor; rather, larger schemas increase the probability of including high-SCS pairs, which contribute disproportionately to confusion. This distinction has practical implications: a schema of 80 types with low mutual confusability may outperform a 40-type schema with clustered semantics, and suggests that confusability-aware design principles may generalize to any task where an LLM must discriminate among a prompt-specified label inventory.\n\n\\section{Additional Experimental Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_additional_details}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Seed Variance Decomposition}\n\\label{app:variance-decomposition}\n\nTo quantify whether performance variability is attributable to random batch composition or seed-dependent model inference noise, we decompose total variance into intra-seed (batch) and inter-seed components across three seeds (42, 123, 456), each evaluated on 25 random batches per count level.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Batch SD} & \\textbf{Seed SD} & \\textbf{Ratio} \\\\\n\\midrule\n4 & 0.0889 & 0.0122 & 53.5 \\\\\n8 & 0.0820 & 0.0098 & 70.0 \\\\\n16 & 0.0626 & 0.0270 & 5.4 \\\\\n32 & 0.0462 & 0.0091 & 26.0 \\\\\n40 & 0.0421 & 0.0019 & 474.4 \\\\\n64 & 0.0245 & 0.0022 & 119.8 \\\\\n80 & 0.0001 & 0.0088 & $<$0.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition across count levels on FewRel. Batch SD is the root-mean intra-seed variance across 25 random type batches (averaged over 3 seeds). Seed SD is the standard deviation of the 3 per-seed means. Ratio = mean batch variance / seed variance. At count 80, only one batch composition exists (the full schema), so batch variance vanishes by construction.}\n\\label{tab:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Batch SD} & \\textbf{Seed SD} & \\textbf{Ratio} \\\\\n\\midrule\n2 & 0.1782 & 0.0557 & 10.2 \\\\\n6 & 0.1233 & 0.0160 & 59.2 \\\\\n12 & 0.1063 & 0.0297 & 12.8 \\\\\n22 & 0.0954 & 0.0096 & 99.2 \\\\\n44 & 0.1214 & 0.0143 & 72.4 \\\\\n66 & 0.0016 & 0.0002 & 46.3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition on Few-NERD (3 seeds $\\times$ 25 batches per count level). Batch SD: root-mean intra-seed standard deviation averaged over seeds. Seed SD: standard deviation of per-seed means. Ratio = batch variance / seed variance. At count 66 (full schema), only one composition exists, so both components collapse to near-zero.}\n\\label{tab:variance_decomposition_ner}\n\\end{table}\n\nIntra-batch variance dominates inter-seed variance by 5--474$\\times$ on FewRel (mean 125$\\times$, counts 4--64) and 10--99$\\times$ on Few-NERD (mean 51$\\times$, counts 2--44), confirming that random batch composition is the primary source of performance variability on both tasks. Under greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$), model inference is deterministic; the inter-seed variance reflects differences in which random batch compositions each seed generates, not inference stochasticity. The wide ratio range reflects variation in how tightly seed means cluster at each count level; even the minimum ratio (5.4$\\times$ on FewRel at count 16, 10.2$\\times$ on Few-NERD at count 2) indicates batch composition contributes the majority of observed variance. At count 80 (FewRel) and count 66 (Few-NERD), the full schema admits only one batch composition, collapsing batch variance by construction.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
