38	\section*{Limitations}
39	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
40	\item \textbf{Model coverage.} Primary experiments use Qwen3-32B with cross-model validation on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. Whether patterns hold for proprietary models (GPT-4, Gemini) remains unverified. Dimension~2 was not robustly detectable on NER at 8B scale, likely due to floor effects.
41	
42	\item \textbf{Modest pairwise effect size.} Pairwise SCS explains 1.3--1.7\% of type confusion variance; entity frequency, prompt position, mention ambiguity, and type definition quality likely account for the remainder.
43	
44	\item \textbf{Factorial interaction tests.} Within-dataset count $\times$ BCS interaction on Few-NERD did not reach significance ($p = 0.50$; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}). However, a FewRel factorial with TOST equivalence testing confirms bounded association ($p_{\text{TOST}} = 0.003$ at $\pm 3$\,pp, interaction = 0.47\,pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}), and cross-pool slope consistency ($p = 0.60$) corroborates stable rather than amplifying sensitivity.
45	
46	\item \textbf{Decomposition and metric scope.} RE batch composition is tested on FewRel only; NER merge alternatives remain unexplored. SCS relies on embedding cosine similarity; label entropy or ontological depth may capture complementary dimensions. For Few-NERD, SCS is computed from the extraction model's own embeddings (Qwen3), creating a shared-representation dependency. MiniLM cross-check yields a conservative but directionally consistent estimate ($r = 0.172$ vs $r = 0.138$; confusion matrix correlation 0.894), suggesting the effect is not an artifact of shared representations.
47	
48	\item \textbf{NER evaluation metric.} Our exact-match NER evaluation conflates boundary errors (partial span overlap) with type errors (correct span, wrong label). Relaxed matching may yield different confusability effect sizes, as boundary ambiguity may correlate with schema complexity independently of type similarity.
49	
50	\item \textbf{RE format specificity.} RE's clean dimension separation may reflect its closed-set classification format. FewRel combines training and validation splits for 80 types and characterizes classification accuracy rather than end-to-end extraction.
51	
52	\item \textbf{Causal mechanism.} The study establishes correlational evidence with controlled stratification; mechanistic analysis at the attention or logit level is needed to confirm the causal pathway. The controlled stratification provides evidence consistent with a directional relationship but does not establish interventional causality.
53	
54	\item \textbf{Data contamination.} FewRel (2018) may appear in pretraining corpora. However, train-origin relations show numerically lower accuracy than val-origin ($-8.8$\,pp, $p=0.06$, n.s.), opposite to the contamination hypothesis. Moreover, the monotonic count-dependent degradation curve (85.7\%$\to$50.4\%) cannot be explained by uniform contamination, which would elevate all conditions equally. BCS slope consistency across independent and within-pool conditions (permutation $p = 0.60$) provides a robustness check that is difficult to reconcile with non-uniform contamination, which would differentially affect specific type subsets. DocRED (2019) provides a lower-contamination-risk replication with consistent BCS--accuracy slopes ($R^2 = 0.53$). Adding train-origin ratio as a covariate leaves the BCS partial effect unchanged ($p = 0.245$ for origin; Appendix~\ref{app:split-origin}).
55	\end{enumerate}
56	
57	\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} Cross-dataset comparisons (FewRel, DocRED, Few-NERD, MAVEN) serve as independent replications of the same hypothesis and do not require family-wise correction \citep{rothman-1990}. Within-dataset secondary analyses (e.g., quartile comparisons, slope sensitivity checks) involve multiple looks at the same data; we report individual $p$-values and note that core claims rest on effects reaching $p < 0.001$.

124	\section{SDGD Full Protocol and Results}
125	\label{sec:appendix_sdgd}
126	
127	We construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\textsc{largest\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent (i.e., highly confusable) types share a color. Each color class forms one extraction batch, and predictions are merged via set union. We compare four modes: (i)~\textbf{single\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\textbf{sdgd\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\textbf{random\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\textbf{per\_type}, the extreme case where each type is extracted in isolation.
128	
129	Single-pass extraction achieves F1\,=\,0.445 (P\,=\,0.563, R\,=\,0.368). SDGD obtains F1\,=\,0.251 (P\,=\,0.210, R\,=\,0.314), a 44\% relative drop. Random batching reaches F1\,=\,0.320 (P\,=\,0.249, R\,=\,0.446), and per-type extraction yields F1\,=\,0.292 (P\,=\,0.194, R\,=\,0.586). Every decomposition mode falls below the single-pass baseline (Table~\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).
130	
131	\begin{table}[h]
132	\centering
133	\small
134	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
135	\begin{tabular}{lrccc}
136	\toprule
137	\textbf{Mode} & \textbf{\# Batches} & \textbf{Precision} & \textbf{Recall} & \textbf{F1} \\
138	\midrule
139	single\_pass & 1 & 0.563 & 0.368 & 0.445 \\
140	\midrule
141	sdgd\_zero (v1) & 8 & 0.210 & 0.314 & 0.251 \\
142	random\_batch (v1) & 8 & 0.249 & 0.446 & 0.320 \\
143	per\_type & 66 & 0.194 & 0.586 & 0.292 \\
144	\midrule
145	sdgd\_zero (v2) & 4 & 0.324 & 0.191 & 0.241 \\
146	random\_batch (v2) & 8 & 0.331 & 0.276 & 0.301 \\
147	\bottomrule
148	\end{tabular}}
149	\caption{Few-NERD extraction under decomposition modes. V1 uses union merge; v2 uses confidence-gated merge with \texttt{min\_batch\_size=8} (reducing 8$\to$4 batches for sdgd\_zero). All decomposition modes produce lower F1 than the single-pass baseline. All modes use the v1 prompt.}
150	\label{tab:sdgd_results}
151	\end{table}
152	
153	Decomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions, inflating false positives.
154	
155	Our v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\_zero: 0.210$\to$0.324; random\_batch: 0.249$\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\to$0.191; 0.446$\to$0.276), yielding lower F1 in both modes (sdgd\_zero: 0.251$\to$0.241; random\_batch: 0.320$\to$0.301).
156

158	\label{sec:appendix_routing}
159	
160	We evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition (same grouping as Table~\ref{tab:batch_composition}). Each strategy selects a single sub-schema for each test instance and returns that sub-schema's prediction.

The failure stems from constrained decoding (enum constraint), which forces confident predictions in every sub-schema: 73.4\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, with per-sub-schema none-rates of only 20--40\%.}

183	
184	\section{Factorial Analysis and Separability Test}
185	\label{sec:appendix_factorial}
186	
187	To formally test whether count and confusability effects are additive, we fit a multiple regression (accuracy $\sim$ $\log_2$\,count $+$ BCS $+$ count$\times$BCS) across 69 batches from FewRel spanning five count levels ($k \in \{4, 8, 16, 32, 40\}$). Both main effects are significant (count: $p = 0.008$; BCS: $p = 0.0002$; $R^2 = 0.52$), but the interaction term is negligible (0.47\,pp). A TOST equivalence test with a $\pm 3$\,pp margin confirms that the interaction is bounded well below practical significance ($p_{\text{TOST}} = 0.003$), supporting bounded association between count and confusability effects.
188	
189	A $2 \times 2$ factorial sensitivity check on FewRel (count $\in \{8, 16\}$, BCS low/high; $n = 54$) was inconclusive ($p_{\text{TOST}} = 0.56$) due to limited power; this does not contradict the primary finding but limits generalization beyond the regression framework. The Few-NERD factorial (\S\ref{sec:multi_channel}) similarly did not reach significance ($p = 0.50$). The regression approach provides the stronger test by leveraging the full count range.
190	
191	\paragraph{Equivalence margin justification.}
192	We set the $\pm 3$\,pp margin based on three converging anchors. \emph{Empirical}: typical random-batch confusability variation produces 2--5\,pp accuracy differences (batch SD $\approx 4.6$\,pp at count 32). \emph{Practical}: a ${<}3$\,pp accuracy difference falls below deployment-relevant thresholds. \emph{Statistical}: 3\,pp $\approx 0.5 \times$ batch SD, equivalent to Cohen's $d \approx 0.5$ (medium effect threshold; \citealt{lakens-2017-equivalence}). A 3\,pp interaction atop the 2.2\,pp confusability main effect would represent ${>}130\%$ amplification, fundamentally changing whether confusability warrants mitigation. At $\pm 2$\,pp (marginally equivalent, $p = 0.047$), interaction remains bounded below the main effect itself.
193	
194	\begin{table}[h]
195	\centering
196	\small
197	\begin{tabular}{crrl}
198	\toprule
199	\textbf{Margin (pp)} & \textbf{$t_{\text{upper}}$} & \textbf{$p_{\text{TOST}}$} & \textbf{Conclusion} \\
200	\midrule
201	$\pm 1$ & $-0.59$ & $0.278$ & Inconclusive \\
202	$\pm 2$ & $-1.70$ & $0.047$ & Equivalent \\
203	$\pm 3$ & $-2.81$ & $0.003$ & Equivalent \\
204	$\pm 5$ & $-5.02$ & ${<}\,0.001$ & Equivalent \\
205	\bottomrule
206	\end{tabular}
207	\caption{TOST sensitivity analysis across equivalence margins. The interaction effect (0.47\,pp, 90\% CI [$-$1.0, 2.0]\,pp) is bounded below 2\,pp at marginal significance ($p = 0.047$), confirming that the $\pm 3$\,pp choice does not drive the equivalence conclusion. At $\pm 1$\,pp, equivalence is inconclusive ($p = 0.278$), meaning interaction could reach approximately half the main effect magnitude.}
208	\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}
209	\end{table}
210	
211	\section{Within-Pool CI Details}
212	\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}
213	
214	The quartile $\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\,pp, 95\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (CI: [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.
215	
216	\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}
217	\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}
218	
219	Section~\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.
220	
221	\paragraph{Confound identification.}
222	For each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:
223	\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]
224	\item \textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, producing the positive BCS--F1 association.
225	\item \textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors, central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.
226	\end{itemize}
227	
228	\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}
229	Table~\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.
230	
231	\begin{table}[h]
232	\centering
233	\small
234	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
235	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
236	\toprule
237	\textbf{Embedding} & \textbf{Control} & \textbf{Raw $\rho$} & \textbf{Raw $p$} & \textbf{Partial $r$} & \textbf{Partial $p$} \\
238	\midrule
239	MiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & $0.011$ & --- & --- \\
240	MiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & $0.11$ \\
241	MiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.23$ \\
242	\midrule
243	Qwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & $0.025$ & --- & --- \\
244	Qwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & $0.44$ \\
245	Qwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.44$ \\
246	\bottomrule
247	\end{tabular}}
248	\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \texttt{exp\_maven\_residualized\_bcs}.}
249	\label{tab:residualized_bcs}
250	\end{table}
251	
252	\paragraph{Interpretation.}
253	The two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality (the count of strong semantic neighbors) becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.
254	
255	Under joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$, confirming that the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.
256	
257	\section{Batch Composition Results}
258	\label{sec:appendix_batch_composition}
259	
260	See Table~\ref{tab:batch_composition} and the accompanying discussion in \S\ref{sec:sdgd}.
261	
262	\input{prompt_appendix}
263	
264	\section{Prompt Version Sensitivity Analysis}
265	\label{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}
266	
267	We assess whether the confusability effects reported in \S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}--\ref{sec:multi_channel} are robust to prompt design by replicating key experiments under the v2 prompt (\S\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).
268	
269	\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}
270	Under the v1 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap (Table~\ref{tab:bcs_controlled}) is substantial. Under v2, with $n{=}25$ batches per group and wider BCS contrast (low-BCS range [0.356, 0.367]; high-BCS range [0.791, 0.811]), the gap is $-$25.5\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\% vs.\ high-BCS 59.7\%; 95\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $p < 0.001$; Pearson $r = -0.919$). The effect is significant, directionally consistent with v1, and only modestly attenuated in magnitude ($-$5.7\,pp reduction). An earlier $n{=}5$ evaluation with narrower BCS contrast (0.21) yielded a smaller gap of $-$7.9\,pp (CI: [$-$5.2, $+$21.5]), illustrating how limited statistical power and restricted BCS range can obscure the underlying signal, a cautionary note for small-sample prompt sensitivity studies.
271	
272	\paragraph{NER (Few-NERD).}
273	The batch-level BCS--F1 correlation under v2 is consistent with v1: at count${}\,=\,12$, $r = +0.58$ ($p = 0.002$, 95\% CI: [0.19, 0.81]) vs.\ v1 $r = +0.50$ ($p = 0.008$). As discussed in \S\ref{sec:pairwise}, this positive direction reflects a type-frequency confound at the batch level (high-BCS batches contain high-frequency entity types that models extract more reliably) rather than evidence against the confusability hypothesis. The pairwise SCS--confusion analysis (\S\ref{sec:pairwise}), which is immune to this aggregation artifact, remains the primary evidence for the NER confusability dimension.
274	
275	\paragraph{Interpretation.}
276	The $n{=}25$ v2 result confirms that the confusability effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude: the gap narrows ($-$5.7\,pp) but remains highly significant ($p < 0.001$, $r = -0.919$). The earlier $n{=}5$ non-significance reflected insufficient power and narrow BCS contrast, not absence of the effect. V2's stricter format constraints raise the baseline accuracy (low-BCS 85.2\% vs.\ 82.3\%), but type confusion under high confusability persists (59.7\% vs.\ 51.0\%). Prompt design modulates the practical magnitude of the confusability effect but does not eliminate it, ruling out prompt-specific confounding as the primary source. The underlying confusability signal (pairwise SCS--confusion correlation and the directional BCS--accuracy relationship) persists across both prompt versions.
277	
278	\subsection{Cross-Prompt-Variant BCS Correlation}
279	\label{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}
280	
281	To verify that the BCS--accuracy relationship is not an artifact of a specific prompt template, we evaluate four structurally distinct prompt variants on FewRel 80-relation RE at two count levels (8 and 32), with 10 random batches per condition.
282	
283	\begin{table}[h]
284	\centering
285	\small
286	\begin{tabular}{llrrrr}
287	\toprule
288	\textbf{Variant} & \textbf{Count} & \textbf{Mean Acc} & \textbf{$r$} & \textbf{$p$} \\
289	\midrule
290	V2 (definition-first) & 8 & 0.769 & $-$0.333 & 0.348 \\
291	V2 (definition-first) & 32 & 0.564 & $-$0.648 & 0.043 \\
292	Instruction-rewrite & 8 & 0.847 & $-$0.223 & 0.536 \\
293	Instruction-rewrite & 32 & 0.662 & $-$0.469 & 0.171 \\
294	Chain-of-thought & 8 & 0.749 & $+$0.254 & 0.478 \\
295	Chain-of-thought & 32 & 0.617 & $-$0.566 & 0.088 \\
296	Minimal & 8 & 0.852 & $-$0.170 & 0.638 \\
297	Minimal & 32 & 0.615 & $-$0.771 & 0.009 \\
298	\bottomrule
299	\end{tabular}
300	\caption{BCS--accuracy Pearson correlation across four prompt variants. At count 32, all four variants show negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r \leq -0.47$), with two reaching $p < 0.05$. At count 8, the narrower BCS range reduces statistical power.}
301	\label{tab:prompt_variants}
302	\end{table}
303	
304	At count 32, all four variants produce negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r$ ranges from $-$0.47 to $-$0.77), with V2 ($p = 0.043$) and Minimal ($p = 0.009$) reaching significance and Chain-of-thought approaching it ($p = 0.088$). At count 8, correlations are weaker and nonsignificant across all variants, consistent with the narrower BCS range at lower schema complexity reducing the detectable effect size. These results confirm that the confusability effect arises from schema composition, not prompt-specific phrasing.
305	
306	\section{BCS Aggregation Sensitivity}
307	\label{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}
308	
309	We define Batch Confusability Score (BCS) as the \emph{mean} pairwise SCS across all type pairs in a batch (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). An alternative definition takes the \emph{maximum} pairwise SCS within each batch, capturing the single most confusable pair. Here we compare both aggregation methods to verify that core conclusions are robust to this choice.
310	
311	\begin{table}[h]
312	\centering
313	\small
314	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
315	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
316	\toprule
317	\textbf{Aggregation} & \textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Slope} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{$r$} & \textbf{$p$} \\
318	\midrule
319	MEAN (primary) & 16-rel & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\,0.001$ \\
320	MEAN & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\
321	\midrule
322	MAX & 16-rel & $-$0.449 & [$-$0.552, $-$0.346] & $-$0.637 & ${<}\,0.001$ \\
323	MAX & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\
324	\bottomrule
325	\end{tabular}}
326	\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p \approx 0.60$; MAX $p \approx 0.44$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}
327	\label{tab:bcs_aggregation}
328	\end{table}
329	
330	MAX aggregation yields stronger correlations ($r = -0.637$ vs.\ $-0.467$ for 16-rel), consistent with the hypothesis that the single most confusable pair in a batch predicts substitution errors. Both definitions produce significant negative slopes across all conditions, and the permutation tests confirm no significant difference between independent and sampled pools under either definition. We adopt MEAN as the primary definition because it better represents average pairwise difficulty across all schema combinations, while MAX may overweight a single outlier pair. The choice does not affect any paper conclusion.
331

The 16-relation condition used a single extreme batch per BCS level; bootstrap CIs require multiple batches.}

352	\section{Within-Pool Analysis Methodology}
353	\label{app:within-pool-method}
354	
355	A potential confound in the cross-scale comparison is that the 16-relation and 80-relation pools contain different types, so the observed gap could reflect pool composition rather than confusability. To control for this, we draw 100 random 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool and evaluate under the same BCS-controlled protocol.
356	
357	\begin{table}[h]
358	\centering
359	\small
360	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
361	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}
362	\toprule
363	& \multicolumn{4}{c}{\textbf{Regression (BCS $\to$ Accuracy)}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Quartile $\Delta$}} \\
364	\cmidrule(lr){2-5} \cmidrule(lr){6-7}
365	\textbf{Setting} & \textbf{Slope} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{$r$} & \textbf{$p$} & \textbf{$\Delta$ (pp)} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
366	\midrule
367	Independent 16-rel ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\,0.001$ & $-$5.31 & [$-$7.44, $-$3.19] \\
368	80-pool $\to$ 16-sub ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & $0.027$ & $-$3.92 & [$-$8.17, $+$0.38] \\
369	Full 80-rel ($n{=}25$)$^\dagger$ & $+$0.221 & [$-$1.303, $+$1.695] & $+$0.064 & $0.76$ & $-$1.04 & [$-$10.12, $+$8.04] \\
370	\midrule
371	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\footnotesize Permutation test (16-rel vs.\ 80-pool slopes): $p \approx 0.60$ (5{,}000 permutations)} \\
372	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\footnotesize $^\dagger$BCS range (IQR\,=\,0.033) insufficient for regression; slope not interpretable.} \\

376	\label{tab:within_pool}
377	\end{table}
378	
379	The within-pool slope ($-$0.401, CI excludes zero) is statistically indistinguishable from the independent 16-relation pool ($-$0.509; permutation $p \approx 0.60$). The smaller quartile gap ($-$3.92\,pp vs.\ $-$5.31\,pp) reflects the narrower BCS range within the 80-relation pool (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool}).
380	
381	In the full 80-relation condition, natural BCS variation is insufficient (IQR${}=0.033$) to yield a detectable effect ($n=25$, slope${}=+0.221$, $p \approx 0.76$). This does not invalidate the confusability effect; the within-pool slope is significant ($p=0.027$) when BCS is deliberately varied, but establishes that natural schema sampling at this scale does not produce sufficient BCS contrast for detection.
382

404	Few-NERD is the only dataset whose prompts include type definitions; for MAVEN and FewRel, prompts use type names alone, making name-only SCS a direct match to model input. The high matrix correlation (0.66--0.89) confirms that type names capture the dominant semantic structure.
405	
406	\section{Pair-to-Batch Aggregation Simulation}
407	\label{app:aggregation-sim}
408	
409	The following simulation provides an illustrative explanatory model for how pair-level effects aggregate to batch-level variance. It is not the evidence base for our claims; the observed batch-level $R^2 = 0.218$ (\S\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}) is a direct empirical measurement independent of this simulation.
410	
411	\paragraph{Setup.} We examine whether the pair-level $R^2 \approx 7\%$ (computed over non-zero confusion pairs) can aggregate to the observed batch-level $R^2 \approx 22\%$ via a Monte Carlo simulation. From the FewRel 16-relation experiment, we estimate the inter-pair SCS correlation for pairs sharing a common type ($\rho_\text{SCS} = 0.35$). For batches of $k=8$ types ($\binom{8}{2}=28$ pairs), we simulate 10{,}000 iterations: (1) generate 28 correlated pair-level effects with individual $R^2 = 0.074$; (2) average to obtain batch-level signals; (3) compute batch-level $R^2$.
412	
413	\paragraph{Results.} Simulated batch $R^2 = 0.219$ [95\% CI: 0.092--0.361], matching the observed value of 0.218. The effective amplification factor is $\sim$3$\times$, explained by signal correlation within batches preserving variance while independent noise averages out (effective degrees of freedom $\approx 3.4$, not 28).
414	
415	\paragraph{Unconditional baseline.}
416	When the simulation uses the unconditional pairwise $r^2 = 0.013$ (computed over all type pairs including zero-confusion pairs; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}), the simulated batch-level $R^2$ drops to $0.044$ [95\% CI: 0.001--0.151], well below the observed $R^2 = 0.218$. The analytical formula confirms: $\text{SNR} \times \text{EF} / (\text{SNR} \times \text{EF} + 1) = 0.042$ with the same effective factor $\text{EF} = 3.35$. This ${\sim}5\times$ gap reflects the dilution from zero-confusion pairs: approximately 89\% of type pairs never exhibit mutual confusion, contributing only noise to the unconditional correlation. The conditional model ($R^2 = 0.074 \to 0.219$) matches the observed value because it isolates the minority of semantically proximate pairs that actually compete during type resolution; BCS predicts batch accuracy through the density of \emph{active} confusable pairs, not uniform contributions from all pairs.
417	
418	\section{Split-Origin Covariate Analysis}
419	\label{app:split-origin}
420	
421	FewRel combines 64 train-split and 16 val-split relation types into a single 80-type pool.  Train-origin types show numerically lower accuracy ($-8.8$\,pp, $p = 0.06$; see Limitations), raising the question of whether differential pre-training exposure confounds the BCS--accuracy relationship.  We test this by adding a per-batch \emph{train-origin ratio} (fraction of relation types drawn from the train split) as a covariate in the BCS--accuracy regression.
422	
423	\paragraph{Method.}  Across 213 multi-type batches ($k \geq 2$) from the batch-composition experiment (\S\ref{sec:sdgd}) and the factorial design (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}), we compute $\texttt{train\_origin\_ratio} = n_{\text{train}} / k$ for each batch.  The ratio ranges from 0.40 to 1.00 (mean 0.80, reflecting the 64/80 base rate).  We fit:
424	\[
425	\text{accuracy} = \beta_0 + \beta_{\text{BCS}} \cdot \text{BCS} + \beta_{\text{origin}} \cdot \text{train\_origin\_ratio} + \varepsilon
426	\]
427	
428	\paragraph{Results.}  The BCS partial effect is unchanged after controlling for split-origin: $\beta_{\text{BCS}} = -0.416$, $p < 10^{-16}$ (vs.\ $\beta = -0.412$, $p < 10^{-16}$ without the covariate).  Train-origin ratio is not independently significant ($\beta_{\text{origin}} = -0.057$, $p = 0.245$), and $\Delta R^2 = 0.005$.  The BCS--accuracy relationship is therefore not confounded by differential pre-training exposure at the batch level.
429	
430	Pairwise SCS distributions do not differ significantly between train-origin (64 types, mean\,=\,0.880) and val-origin (16 types, mean\,=\,0.874) subsets (Mann-Whitney $p = 0.754$, Cohen's $d = 0.081$), confirming that the train/val split does not introduce systematic confusability bias.
431	
432	\section{Cross-Seed Variance Decomposition}
433	\label{app:variance-decomposition}
434	
435	\begin{table}[h]
436	\centering
437	\small
438	\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
439	\toprule
440	\textbf{Count} & \textbf{Batch SD} & \textbf{Seed SD} & \textbf{Ratio} \\
441	\midrule
442	4 & 0.0889 & 0.0122 & 53.5 \\
443	8 & 0.0820 & 0.0098 & 70.0 \\
444	16 & 0.0626 & 0.0270 & 5.4 \\
445	32 & 0.0462 & 0.0091 & 26.0 \\
446	40 & 0.0421 & 0.0019 & 474.4 \\
447	64 & 0.0245 & 0.0022 & 119.8 \\
448	80 & 0.0001 & 0.0088 & $<$0.1 \\
449	\bottomrule
450	\end{tabular}
451	\caption{Variance decomposition on FewRel (3 seeds $\times$ 25 batches per count level). Batch SD: root-mean intra-seed variance averaged over seeds. Seed SD: standard deviation of per-seed means. Ratio = batch variance / seed variance. At count 80, only one batch composition exists, so batch variance vanishes by construction.}
452	\label{tab:variance_decomposition}
453	\end{table}
454	
455	Intra-batch variance dominates inter-seed variance by 5--474$\times$ across count levels 4--64 (mean 125$\times$), confirming that random batch composition is the primary source of performance variability. Under greedy decoding (temperature${}\,=\,0$), model inference is deterministic; the inter-seed variance reflects differences in which random batch compositions each seed generates, not inference stochasticity. At count 80, the full schema admits only one composition, leaving model inference noise as the sole variance source (seed SD = 0.88\,pp).
456	
457	\section{Cross-Domain Connections}
458	\label{app:cross-domain}
459	
460	The schema-induced degradation documented here is not unique to information extraction. Schema-Guided Dialogue systems (SGD/SGD-X; \citealt{rastogi2020towards}) face an analogous challenge: as the number of intent and slot types grows, dialogue state tracking accuracy declines. \citet{li2023evaluating} observed count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT-based IE, attributing it to prompt overload. Our two-dimensional decomposition offers a more refined explanation: schema size per se is not the sole predictor; rather, larger schemas increase the probability of including high-SCS pairs, which contribute disproportionately to confusion. This distinction has practical implications: a schema of 80 types with low mutual confusability may outperform a 40-type schema with clustered semantics, and suggests that confusability-aware design principles may generalize to any task where an LLM must discriminate among a prompt-specified label inventory.
461	
462	\end{document}

Within-dataset secondary analyses (e.g., quartile comparisons, slope sensitivity checks) involve multiple looks at the same data; we report individual $p$-values and note that core claims rest on effects reaching $p < 0.001$.\n\n\\input{checklist}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{sec:appendix_figures}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B} & \\textbf{Qwen3-32B} \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & 0.702 & 0.640 \\\\\n64 & 0.273 & 0.304 \\\\\n\\midrule\nDecline & $-$43\\,pp & $-$34\\,pp \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-model count-curve replication on Few-NERD (text-match recall). Both models show a general declining trend in recall with schema size. Per-batch correlation $r = 0.725$. Qwen3-32B values use the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}) for fair cross-model comparison; the count-curve results in \\S\\ref{sec:count_curves} use the v1 prompt, yielding higher absolute recall (0.72 vs.\\ 0.64 at count${}\\,=\\,1$) due to over-prediction under v1.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{crrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Qwen3-32B}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n8 & 78.1\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$27.1 & 82.3\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$31.2 \\\\\n16 & 75.5\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$23.9 & 82.8\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$31.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Cross-model BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy.  High-BCS accuracy converges to $\\approx$51\\% regardless of model, while low-BCS accuracy reflects model capacity differences.  The confusability gap ($\\Delta$, in pp) is 77--87\\% as large on Llama-3.1-8B as on Qwen3-32B.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n & & \\multicolumn{5}{c}{\\textbf{BCS Bin}} & & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-7}\n\\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{Error} & \\textbf{Solo} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{Mid} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{V.High} & \\textbf{$\\rho$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nNER & IntraSub & 0\\% & 1.6\\% & 10.6\\% & 49.4\\% & --- & 1.000 & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nRE  & IntraSub & 0\\% & 4.2\\% & 13.0\\% & 20.3\\% & 32.3\\% & 0.954 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nNER & Missed & --- & 42.8\\% & 64.8\\% & 49.9\\% & --- & --- & --- \\\\\nRE  & Missed & --- & 10.4\\% & 11.3\\% & 13.1\\% & 15.7\\% & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) and missed-entity rates by BCS bin, with Spearman $\\rho$ computed over $n=154$ individual batches (RE) and $n=148$ batches (NER). NER has 4 BCS bins; RE has 5.}\n\\label{tab:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{SDGD Full Protocol and Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_sdgd}\n\nWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent (i.e., highly confusable) types share a color. Each color class forms one extraction batch, and predictions are merged via set union. We compare four modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, the extreme case where each type is extracted in isolation.\n\nSingle-pass extraction achieves F1\\,=\\,0.445 (P\\,=\\,0.563, R\\,=\\,0.368). SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251 (P\\,=\\,0.210, R\\,=\\,0.314), a 44\\% relative drop. Random batching reaches F1\\,=\\,0.320 (P\\,=\\,0.249, R\\,=\\,0.446), and per-type extraction yields F1\\,=\\,0.292 (P\\,=\\,0.194, R\\,=\\,0.586). Every decomposition mode falls below the single-pass baseline (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Mode} & \\textbf{\\# Batches} & \\textbf{Precision} & \\textbf{Recall} & \\textbf{F1} \\\\\n\\midrule\nsingle\\_pass & 1 & 0.563 & 0.368 & 0.445 \\\\\n\\midrule\nsdgd\\_zero (v1) & 8 & 0.210 & 0.314 & 0.251 \\\\\nrandom\\_batch (v1) & 8 & 0.249 & 0.446 & 0.320 \\\\\nper\\_type & 66 & 0.194 & 0.586 & 0.292 \\\\\n\\midrule\nsdgd\\_zero (v2) & 4 & 0.324 & 0.191 & 0.241 \\\\\nrandom\\_batch (v2) & 8 & 0.331 & 0.276 & 0.301 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Few-NERD extraction under decomposition modes. V1 uses union merge; v2 uses confidence-gated merge with \\texttt{min\\_batch\\_size=8} (reducing 8$\\to$4 batches for sdgd\\_zero). All decomposition modes produce lower F1 than the single-pass baseline. All modes use the v1 prompt.}\n\\label{tab:sdgd_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nDecomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions, inflating false positives.\n\nOur v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\\_zero: 0.210$\\to$0.324; random\\_batch: 0.249$\\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\\to$0.191; 0.446$\\to$0.276), yielding lower F1 in both modes (sdgd\\_zero: 0.251$\\to$0.241; random\\_batch: 0.320$\\to$0.301).\n\n\\subsection{Practical Routing Strategies on RE}\n\\label{sec:appendix_routing}\n\nWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition (same grouping as Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). Each strategy selects a single sub-schema for each test instance and returns that sub-schema's prediction.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Strategy} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ Single-Pass} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle (gold label) & 72.4\\% & +22.0\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nMax-conf (positive only) & 36.5\\% & $-$14.0\\,pp \\\\\nNone-aware & 36.5\\% & $-$14.0\\,pp \\\\\nMargin (positive only) & 35.9\\% & $-$14.5\\,pp \\\\\nMax-conf (all) & 27.7\\% & $-$22.7\\,pp \\\\\nRandom & 14.7\\% & $-$35.7\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (no decomposition) & 50.4\\% & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Routing strategy comparison on FewRel 80-relation RE with graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition. Oracle requires gold labels. All practical (non-oracle) strategies perform below the single-pass baseline. The failure stems from constrained decoding (enum constraint), which forces confident predictions in every sub-schema: 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, with per-sub-schema none-rates of only 20--40\\%.}\n\\label{tab:routing_strategies}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Factorial Analysis and Separability Test}\n\\label{sec:appendix_factorial}\n\nTo formally test whether count and confusability effects are additive, we fit a multiple regression (accuracy $\\sim$ $\\log_2$\\,count $+$ BCS $+$ count$\\times$BCS) across 69 batches from FewRel spanning five count levels ($k \\in \\{4, 8, 16, 32, 40\\}$). Both main effects are significant (count: $p = 0.008$; BCS: $p = 0.0002$; $R^2 = 0.52$), but the interaction term is negligible (0.47\\,pp). A TOST equivalence test with a $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin confirms that the interaction is bounded well below practical significance ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$), supporting bounded association between count and confusability effects.\n\nA $2 \\times 2$ factorial sensitivity check on FewRel (count $\\in \\{8, 16\\}$, BCS low/high; $n = 54$) was inconclusive ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.56$) due to limited power; this does not contradict the primary finding but limits generalization beyond the regression framework. The Few-NERD factorial (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}) similarly did not reach significance ($p = 0.50$). The regression approach provides the stronger test by leveraging the full count range.\n\n\\paragraph{Equivalence margin justification.}\nWe set the $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin based on three converging anchors. \\emph{Empirical}: typical random-batch confusability variation produces 2--5\\,pp accuracy differences (batch SD $\\approx 4.6$\\,pp at count 32). \\emph{Practical}: a ${<}3$\\,pp accuracy difference falls below deployment-relevant thresholds. \\emph{Statistical}: 3\\,pp $\\approx 0.5 \\times$ batch SD, equivalent to Cohen's $d \\approx 0.5$ (medium effect threshold; \\citealt{lakens-2017-equivalence}). A 3\\,pp interaction atop the 2.2\\,pp confusability main effect would represent ${>}130\\%$ amplification, fundamentally changing whether confusability warrants mitigation. At $\\pm 2$\\,pp (marginally equivalent, $p = 0.047$), interaction remains bounded below the main effect itself.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{crrl}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Margin (pp)} & \\textbf{$t_{\\text{upper}}$} & \\textbf{$p_{\\text{TOST}}$} & \\textbf{Conclusion} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\pm 1$ & $-0.59$ & $0.278$ & Inconclusive \\\\\n$\\pm 2$ & $-1.70$ & $0.047$ & Equivalent \\\\\n$\\pm 3$ & $-2.81$ & $0.003$ & Equivalent \\\\\n$\\pm 5$ & $-5.02$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ & Equivalent \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{TOST sensitivity analysis across equivalence margins. The interaction effect (0.47\\,pp, 90\\% CI [$-$1.0, 2.0]\\,pp) is bounded below 2\\,pp at marginal significance ($p = 0.047$), confirming that the $\\pm 3$\\,pp choice does not drive the equivalence conclusion. At $\\pm 1$\\,pp, equivalence is inconclusive ($p = 0.278$), meaning interaction could reach approximately half the main effect magnitude.}\n\\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Within-Pool CI Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}\n\nThe quartile $\\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\\,pp, 95\\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (CI: [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.\n\n\\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \\emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound identification.}\nFor each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:\n\\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, producing the positive BCS--F1 association.\n\\item \\textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors, central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Embedding} & \\textbf{Control} & \\textbf{Raw $\\rho$} & \\textbf{Raw $p$} & \\textbf{Partial $r$} & \\textbf{Partial $p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & $0.011$ & --- & --- \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & $0.11$ \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.23$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & $0.025$ & --- & --- \\\\\nQwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & $0.44$ \\\\\nQwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.44$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \\texttt{exp\\_maven\\_residualized\\_bcs}.}\n\\label{tab:residualized_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality (the count of strong semantic neighbors) becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.\n\nUnder joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$, confirming that the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.\n\n\\section{Batch Composition Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_batch_composition}\n\nSee Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition} and the accompanying discussion in \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}.\n\n\\input{prompt_appendix}\n\n\\section{Prompt Version Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\nWe assess whether the confusability effects reported in \\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}--\\ref{sec:multi_channel} are robust to prompt design by replicating key experiments under the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}\nUnder the v1 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap (Table~\\ref{tab:bcs_controlled}) is substantial. Under v2, with $n{=}25$ batches per group and wider BCS contrast (low-BCS range [0.356, 0.367]; high-BCS range [0.791, 0.811]), the gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; 95\\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $p < 0.001$; Pearson $r = -0.919$). The effect is significant, directionally consistent with v1, and only modestly attenuated in magnitude ($-$5.7\\,pp reduction). An earlier $n{=}5$ evaluation with narrower BCS contrast (0.21) yielded a smaller gap of $-$7.9\\,pp (CI: [$-$5.2, $+$21.5]), illustrating how limited statistical power and restricted BCS range can obscure the underlying signal, a cautionary note for small-sample prompt sensitivity studies.\n\n\\paragraph{NER (Few-NERD).}\nThe batch-level BCS--F1 correlation under v2 is consistent with v1: at count${}\\,=\\,12$, $r = +0.58$ ($p = 0.002$, 95\\% CI: [0.19, 0.81]) vs.\\ v1 $r = +0.50$ ($p = 0.008$). As discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}, this positive direction reflects a type-frequency confound at the batch level (high-BCS batches contain high-frequency entity types that models extract more reliably) rather than evidence against the confusability hypothesis. The pairwise SCS--confusion analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}), which is immune to this aggregation artifact, remains the primary evidence for the NER confusability dimension.\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe $n{=}25$ v2 result confirms that the confusability effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude: the gap narrows ($-$5.7\\,pp) but remains highly significant ($p < 0.001$, $r = -0.919$). The earlier $n{=}5$ non-significance reflected insufficient power and narrow BCS contrast, not absence of the effect. V2's stricter format constraints raise the baseline accuracy (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ 82.3\\%), but type confusion under high confusability persists (59.7\\% vs.\\ 51.0\\%). Prompt design modulates the practical magnitude of the confusability effect but does not eliminate it, ruling out prompt-specific confounding as the primary source. The underlying confusability signal (pairwise SCS--confusion correlation and the directional BCS--accuracy relationship) persists across both prompt versions.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Prompt-Variant BCS Correlation}\n\\label{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}\n\nTo verify that the BCS--accuracy relationship is not an artifact of a specific prompt template, we evaluate four structurally distinct prompt variants on FewRel 80-relation RE at two count levels (8 and 32), with 10 random batches per condition.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Mean Acc} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nV2 (definition-first) & 8 & 0.769 & $-$0.333 & 0.348 \\\\\nV2 (definition-first) & 32 & 0.564 & $-$0.648 & 0.043 \\\\\nInstruction-rewrite & 8 & 0.847 & $-$0.223 & 0.536 \\\\\nInstruction-rewrite & 32 & 0.662 & $-$0.469 & 0.171 \\\\\nChain-of-thought & 8 & 0.749 & $+$0.254 & 0.478 \\\\\nChain-of-thought & 32 & 0.617 & $-$0.566 & 0.088 \\\\\nMinimal & 8 & 0.852 & $-$0.170 & 0.638 \\\\\nMinimal & 32 & 0.615 & $-$0.771 & 0.009 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{BCS--accuracy Pearson correlation across four prompt variants. At count 32, all four variants show negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r \\leq -0.47$), with two reaching $p < 0.05$. At count 8, the narrower BCS range reduces statistical power.}\n\\label{tab:prompt_variants}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt count 32, all four variants produce negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r$ ranges from $-$0.47 to $-$0.77), with V2 ($p = 0.043$) and Minimal ($p = 0.009$) reaching significance and Chain-of-thought approaching it ($p = 0.088$). At count 8, correlations are weaker and nonsignificant across all variants, consistent with the narrower BCS range at lower schema complexity reducing the detectable effect size. These results confirm that the confusability effect arises from schema composition, not prompt-specific phrasing.\n\n\\section{BCS Aggregation Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}\n\nWe define Batch Confusability Score (BCS) as the \\emph{mean} pairwise SCS across all type pairs in a batch (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). An alternative definition takes the \\emph{maximum} pairwise SCS within each batch, capturing the single most confusable pair. Here we compare both aggregation methods to verify that core conclusions are robust to this choice.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Aggregation} & \\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMEAN (primary) & 16-rel & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMEAN & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMAX & 16-rel & $-$0.449 & [$-$0.552, $-$0.346] & $-$0.637 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAX & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p \\approx 0.60$; MAX $p \\approx 0.44$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\nMAX aggregation yields stronger correlations ($r = -0.637$ vs.\\ $-0.467$ for 16-rel), consistent with the hypothesis that the single most confusable pair in a batch predicts substitution errors. Both definitions produce significant negative slopes across all conditions, and the permutation tests confirm no significant difference between independent and sampled pools under either definition. We adopt MEAN as the primary definition because it better represents average pairwise difficulty across all schema combinations, while MAX may overweight a single outlier pair. The choice does not affect any paper conclusion.\n\n\\section{BCS-Controlled Detailed Results}\n\\label{app:bcs-controlled-table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Schema} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Low-BCS} & \\textbf{High-BCS} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\n16-rel & 8 & 88.9\\% & 79.8\\% & $-$9.1 & --- \\\\\n80-rel & 8 & 82.3\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$31.2 & {[}17.7, 42.7{]} \\\\\n80-rel & 16 & 82.8\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$31.2 & {[}22.9, 39.6{]} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. Low-BCS batches contain maximally dissimilar types; high-BCS batches contain maximally similar types. The 80-relation extreme-batch gap reflects the wider BCS range; regression slopes are consistent across pools ($p = 0.60$; Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool}; see \\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude} for typical-batch magnitudes). The 16-relation condition used a single extreme batch per BCS level; bootstrap CIs require multiple batches.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_controlled}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Within-Pool Analysis Methodology}\n\\label{app:within-pool-method}\n\nA potential confound in the cross-scale comparison is that the 16-relation and 80-relation pools contain different types, so the observed gap could reflect pool composition rather than confusability. To control for this, we draw 100 random 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool and evaluate under the same BCS-controlled protocol.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textbf{Regression (BCS $\\to$ Accuracy)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Quartile $\\Delta$}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n\\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIndependent 16-rel ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\\,0.001$ & $-$5.31 & [$-$7.44, $-$3.19] \\\\\n80-pool $\\to$ 16-sub ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & $0.027$ & $-$3.92 & [$-$8.17, $+$0.38] \\\\\nFull 80-rel ($n{=}25$)$^\\dagger$ & $+$0.221 & [$-$1.303, $+$1.695] & $+$0.064 & $0.76$ & $-$1.04 & [$-$10.12, $+$8.04] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize Permutation test (16-rel vs.\\ 80-pool slopes): $p \\approx 0.60$ (5{,}000 permutations)} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$BCS range (IQR\\,=\\,0.033) insufficient for regression; slope not interpretable.} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Three-way BCS--accuracy analysis on FewRel. Slope: OLS of batch accuracy on BCS (bootstrap CIs, $B{=}10{,}000$). $\\Delta$: accuracy gap between top- and bottom-quartile BCS batches. See Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool} for CI interpretation.}\n\\label{tab:within_pool}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe within-pool slope ($-$0.401, CI excludes zero) is statistically indistinguishable from the independent 16-relation pool ($-$0.509; permutation $p \\approx 0.60$). The smaller quartile gap ($-$3.92\\,pp vs.\\ $-$5.31\\,pp) reflects the narrower BCS range within the 80-relation pool (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool}).\n\nIn the full 80-relation condition, natural BCS variation is insufficient (IQR${}=0.033$) to yield a detectable effect ($n=25$, slope${}=+0.221$, $p \\approx 0.76$). This does not invalidate the confusability effect; the within-pool slope is significant ($p=0.027$) when BCS is deliberately varied, but establishes that natural schema sampling at this scale does not produce sufficient BCS contrast for detection.\n\n\\section{SCS Embedding Ablation: Name-Only vs. Name+Definition}\n\\label{app:scs-ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scs-ablation} compares predictive accuracy of name-only SCS against name+definition SCS across three datasets.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nDataset & SCS$_\\text{name}$ $r$ & SCS$_\\text{def}$ $r$ & $\\Delta r$ & Matrix corr. \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & 0.172 & 0.149 & $-$0.023 & 0.894 \\\\\nMAVEN & 0.129 & 0.164 & +0.035 & 0.656 \\\\\nFewRel & 0.178 & 0.193 & +0.015 & 0.848 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Pearson correlation between SCS and empirical confusion rate. ``Def'' concatenates type name with definition before embedding. All correlations significant ($p < 0.001$). The inconsistent direction of $\\Delta r$ indicates no systematic benefit from including definitions.}\n\\label{tab:scs-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nFew-NERD is the only dataset whose prompts include type definitions; for MAVEN and FewRel, prompts use type names alone, making name-only SCS a direct match to model input. The high matrix correlation (0.66--0.89) confirms that type names capture the dominant semantic structure.\n\n\\section{Pair-to-Batch Aggregation Simulation}\n\\label{app:aggregation-sim}\n\nThe following simulation provides an illustrative explanatory model for how pair-level effects aggregate to batch-level variance. It is not the evidence base for our claims; the observed batch-level $R^2 = 0.218$ (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}) is a direct empirical measurement independent of this simulation.\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.} We examine whether the pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ (computed over non-zero confusion pairs) can aggregate to the observed batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ via a Monte Carlo simulation. From the FewRel 16-relation experiment, we estimate the inter-pair SCS correlation for pairs sharing a common type ($\\rho_\\text{SCS} = 0.35$). For batches of $k=8$ types ($\\binom{8}{2}=28$ pairs), we simulate 10{,}000 iterations: (1) generate 28 correlated pair-level effects with individual $R^2 = 0.074$; (2) average to obtain batch-level signals; (3) compute batch-level $R^2$.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.} Simulated batch $R^2 = 0.219$ [95\\% CI: 0.092--0.361], matching the observed value of 0.218. The effective amplification factor is $\\sim$3$\\times$, explained by signal correlation within batches preserving variance while independent noise averages out (effective degrees of freedom $\\approx 3.4$, not 28).\n\n\\paragraph{Unconditional baseline.}\nWhen the simulation uses the unconditional pairwise $r^2 = 0.013$ (computed over all type pairs including zero-confusion pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}), the simulated batch-level $R^2$ drops to $0.044$ [95\\% CI: 0.001--0.151], well below the observed $R^2 = 0.218$. The analytical formula confirms: $\\text{SNR} \\times \\text{EF} / (\\text{SNR} \\times \\text{EF} + 1) = 0.042$ with the same effective factor $\\text{EF} = 3.35$. This ${\\sim}5\\times$ gap reflects the dilution from zero-confusion pairs: approximately 89\\% of type pairs never exhibit mutual confusion, contributing only noise to the unconditional correlation. The conditional model ($R^2 = 0.074 \\to 0.219$) matches the observed value because it isolates the minority of semantically proximate pairs that actually compete during type resolution; BCS predicts batch accuracy through the density of \\emph{active} confusable pairs, not uniform contributions from all pairs.\n\n\\section{Split-Origin Covariate Analysis}\n\\label{app:split-origin}\n\nFewRel combines 64 train-split and 16 val-split relation types into a single 80-type pool.  Train-origin types show numerically lower accuracy ($-8.8$\\,pp, $p = 0.06$; see Limitations), raising the question of whether differential pre-training exposure confounds the BCS--accuracy relationship.  We test this by adding a per-batch \\emph{train-origin ratio} (fraction of relation types drawn from the train split) as a covariate in the BCS--accuracy regression.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}  Across 213 multi-type batches ($k \\geq 2$) from the batch-composition experiment (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}) and the factorial design (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}), we compute $\\texttt{train\\_origin\\_ratio} = n_{\\text{train}} / k$ for each batch.  The ratio ranges from 0.40 to 1.00 (mean 0.80, reflecting the 64/80 base rate).  We fit:\n\\[\n\\text{accuracy} = \\beta_0 + \\beta_{\\text{BCS}} \\cdot \\text{BCS} + \\beta_{\\text{origin}} \\cdot \\text{train\\_origin\\_ratio} + \\varepsilon\n\\]\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}  The BCS partial effect is unchanged after controlling for split-origin: $\\beta_{\\text{BCS}} = -0.416$, $p < 10^{-16}$ (vs.\\ $\\beta = -0.412$, $p < 10^{-16}$ without the covariate).  Train-origin ratio is not independently significant ($\\beta_{\\text{origin}} = -0.057$, $p = 0.245$), and $\\Delta R^2 = 0.005$.  The BCS--accuracy relationship is therefore not confounded by differential pre-training exposure at the batch level.\n\nPairwise SCS distributions do not differ significantly between train-origin (64 types, mean\\,=\\,0.880) and val-origin (16 types, mean\\,=\\,0.874) subsets (Mann-Whitney $p = 0.754$, Cohen's $d = 0.081$), confirming that the train/val split does not introduce systematic confusability bias.\n\n\\section{Cross-Seed Variance Decomposition}\n\\label{app:variance-decomposition}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Batch SD} & \\textbf{Seed SD} & \\textbf{Ratio} \\\\\n\\midrule\n4 & 0.0889 & 0.0122 & 53.5 \\\\\n8 & 0.0820 & 0.0098 & 70.0 \\\\\n16 & 0.0626 & 0.0270 & 5.4 \\\\\n32 & 0.0462 & 0.0091 & 26.0 \\\\\n40 & 0.0421 & 0.0019 & 474.4 \\\\\n64 & 0.0245 & 0.0022 & 119.8 \\\\\n80 & 0.0001 & 0.0088 & $<$0.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition on FewRel (3 seeds $\\times$ 25 batches per count level). Batch SD: root-mean intra-seed variance averaged over seeds. Seed SD: standard deviation of per-seed means. Ratio = batch variance / seed variance. At count 80, only one batch composition exists, so batch variance vanishes by construction.}\n\\label{tab:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{table}\n\nIntra-batch variance dominates inter-seed variance by 5--474$\\times$ across count levels 4--64 (mean 125$\\times$), confirming that random batch composition is the primary source of performance variability. Under greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$), model inference is deterministic; the inter-seed variance reflects differences in which random batch compositions each seed generates, not inference stochasticity. At count 80, the full schema admits only one composition, leaving model inference noise as the sole variance source (seed SD = 0.88\\,pp).\n\n\\section{Cross-Domain Connections}\n\\label{app:cross-domain}\n\nThe schema-induced degradation documented here is not unique to information extraction. Schema-Guided Dialogue systems (SGD/SGD-X; \\citealt{rastogi2020towards}) face an analogous challenge: as the number of intent and slot types grows, dialogue state tracking accuracy declines. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observed count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT-based IE, attributing it to prompt overload. Our two-dimensional decomposition offers a more refined explanation: schema size per se is not the sole predictor; rather, larger schemas increase the probability of including high-SCS pairs, which contribute disproportionately to confusion. This distinction has practical implications: a schema of 80 types with low mutual confusability may outperform a 40-type schema with clustered semantics, and suggests that confusability-aware design principles may generalize to any task where an LLM must discriminate among a prompt-specified label inventory.\n\n\\end{document}\n",
      "numLines": 463,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 463
    }
  }

```text
1	\section{Experimental Setup}
2	\label{sec:experimental_setup}
3	
4	We evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).
5	
6	\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}
7	\label{sec:datasets}
8	
9	\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}
10	Few-NERD \cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances uniformly across test instances; type frequency follows the natural long-tail distribution of Few-NERD. Evaluation uses token-level text matching. Pairwise analysis covers 1{,}953 type pairs with non-zero co-occurrence out of $\binom{66}{2}=2{,}145$ possible pairs.
11	
12	\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}
13	FewRel \cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation.
14	
15	\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}
16	DocRED \cite{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample 1{,}000 instances balanced across 96 relation types ($\sim$10 per type) from the DocRED development set and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.
17	
18	\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}
19	MAVEN \cite{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology, making it a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification.
20	
21	Table~\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \S\ref{sec:scs}.
22	
23	\begin{table}[t]
24	\centering
25	\small
26	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
27	\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}
28	\toprule
29	\textbf{Dataset} & \textbf{Task} & \textbf{\# Types} & \textbf{\# Eval Units} & \textbf{Domain} & \textbf{BCS Range} & \textbf{Embedding} \\
30	\midrule
31	Few-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\
32	FewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\
33	DocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\
34	MAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\rlap{$^\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\
35	\bottomrule
36	\end{tabular}}
37	\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range indicates the min--max Batch Confusability Scores across count-curve batches (Experiment~1). Embedding column indicates the model used for SCS/BCS computation. $^\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences in 710 documents.}
38	\label{tab:datasets}
39	\end{table}
40	
41	\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}
42	\label{sec:scs}
43	
44	We introduce the Schema Confusability Score to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\text{embed}(\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:
45	{\small
46	\[
47	\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \cos\bigl(\text{embed}(\text{name}(t_i)),\; \text{embed}(\text{name}(t_j))\bigr)
48	\]
49	}
50	
51	For a batch of types $B = \{t_1, \ldots, t_k\}$, the \textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:
52	\[
53	\text{BCS}(B) = \frac{1}{\binom{k}{2}} \sum_{i < j,\; t_i, t_j \in B} \text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)
54	\]
55	
56	We compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. Few-NERD uses Qwen3 embeddings because its 66 fine-grained types include same-category pairs (e.g., ``person-artist'' vs.\ ``person-actor'') that MiniLM's 384-d space does not separate well; a cross-embedding check confirms directional consistency (MiniLM $r = 0.172$, Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}). SCS uses name-only embeddings as a practical simplification. We validate this choice empirically: name+definition SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy ($\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\ref{app:scs-ablation}), and for MAVEN/FewRel prompts that use type names without definitions, name-only SCS directly matches prompt content. We use MiniLM as the primary embedding for BCS computation because sentence-transformers are specifically trained for semantic similarity \cite{sentence-bert-2019}; a cross-embedding sensitivity check using Qwen3-32B causal-LM hidden states confirms directional consistency but with compressed BCS range (std${}\,=\,0.034$ vs.\ MiniLM std${}\,=\,0.081$), consistent with known autoregressive anisotropy \cite{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}.
57	
58	\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}
59	\label{sec:scs-validation}
60	SCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on both NER and RE datasets (Few-NERD $r = 0.112$, FewRel $r = 0.187$, both $p < 0.001$; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}). However, SCS is a partial proxy; context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\S\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.
61	
62	\subsection{Experimental Design}
63	\label{sec:exp_design}
64	
65	We conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.
66	
67	\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}
68	We vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\} for Few-NERD, \{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\} for FewRel, \{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\} for DocRED, and \{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\} for MAVEN.
69	
70	\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}
71	We fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\,\in\{6, 12\}$), FewRel (count$\,\in\{8, 16\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\,\in\{16, 32\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).
72	
73	\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}
74	\label{sec:metrics}
75	
76	\textbf{NER}: text-match F1, where a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \textbf{RE}: accuracy (exact match of predicted relation label to gold). \textbf{ED}: relaxed F1, where any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.
77	
78	
79	\subsection{Model and Inference}
80	\label{sec:model}
81	
82	We use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \cite{vllm-2023} on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPU (96\,GB) with greedy decoding (temperature${}\,=\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \cite{llama3-2024}. All experiments use single-seed evaluation with greedy decoding, which eliminates model-level randomness; bootstrap CIs capture the remaining sampling variance from batch composition. Pair-level CIs use percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$); batch-level CIs use $B{=}1{,}000$.
83	
```

Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All experiments use single-seed evaluation with greedy decoding, which eliminates model-level randomness; bootstrap CIs capture the remaining sampling variance from batch composition. Pair-level CIs use percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$); batch-level CIs use $B{=}1{,}000$.\n",
      "numLines": 83,

    }

}

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Training-free LLM IE.}
5	Training-free LLM IE spans instruction tuning \cite{instructuie-2023}, code-style schema encoding \cite{knowcoder-2024,codeie-2023}, annotation-guided extraction \cite{gollie-2024,adelie-2024}, schema-driven decoding \cite{slot-2025,oneke-2025}, and constrained JSON decoding \cite{json-schema-bench-2025}. For NER, methods include zero-shot decomposition \cite{xie-zeroshot-ner-2023,promptner-2023}, distillation \cite{uniner-2024}, and bidirectional encoders \cite{gliner-2024,gliner2-2025}. For RE, recent work examines zero-shot relation triplet extraction via prompting \cite{relationprompt-2022} and revisits relation extraction in the LLM era \cite{wadhwa-re-llm-2023}. Recent work also explores LLM-based data annotation for NER \cite{bogdanov2024nuner,zhang2023llmaaa} and self-improving zero-shot extraction \cite{xie2024selfimproving}. These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.
6	
7	\paragraph{Schema complexity in IE.}
8	The most directly related work is LLM-PEE \cite{llm-pee-2025}, which partitions large event schemas by type embedding similarity. Our work differs in three ways: (1)~we conduct controlled analysis to understand \emph{why} extraction degrades rather than developing an engineering solution; (2)~our BCS-controlled experiments isolate count from confusability, which LLM-PEE conflates; (3)~our SDGD experiments reveal that merge amplification (not partitioning quality) is the bottleneck in decompose-then-merge pipelines. Specifically, LLM-PEE employs union merge across sub-schema extractions, which our \S\ref{sec:sdgd} analysis shows causes precision to collapse from 0.56 to 0.21 in NER as false positives accumulate across passes; RE avoids this because closed-set classification does not require span reconciliation, explaining why LLM-PEE's decomposition succeeds for relation/event tasks but faces unresolved merge challenges on span-level extraction. Other approaches address schema complexity via hard negative mining \cite{iepile-2024}, schema definition optimization \cite{parse-2025}, multi-agent debate \cite{crossagentie-2025,cmas-ner-2025}, schema-aware decomposition for event extraction \cite{dee-2024}, and active prompting \cite{reflect-learn-2026}; all treat it as an engineering problem rather than isolating the underlying degradation factors. Concurrently, \citet{haystack-to-needle-2025} reduces label spaces via iterative LLM filtering, an engineering complement to our analytical approach. Our framework explains \emph{why} such reduction works: it implicitly lowers batch confusability.
9	
10	\paragraph{Schema complexity in dialogue and IE.} The Schema-Guided Dialogue dataset~\cite{rastogi2020towards} and SGD-X~\cite{lee2022sgd} document performance degradation with schema linguistic variation. \citet{li2023evaluating} observe count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT IE. \citet{volkanovska2025ner} categorize LLM NER errors including type confusion. The relationship between label similarity and classification confusion has precedent in traditional ML: hierarchical classification suffers sibling confusion proportional to inter-class proximity \citep{deng-2010-imagenet}, label confusion matrices improve text classification \citep{guo-2021-label-confusion}, and classification confusion captures semantic similarity \citep{zhou-2025-rethinking}. Hierarchical label sets reduce class confusion in zero-shot image classification \citep{novack-2023-chils}; enriched type descriptions improve zero-shot NER \citep{cocchieri-2025-zeroner}, both orthogonal to our confusability-aware batching.
11	
12	\paragraph{SCS novelty.} SCS shares its formal definition (pairwise cosine similarity of type name embeddings) with the partitioning criterion used in LLM-PEE~\cite{llm-pee-2025} for schema decomposition. Our contribution is not the metric itself but the controlled experimental framework that isolates confusability's predictive relationship with extraction degradation, distinguishing it from the engineering use in prior work. The modest pairwise effect size ($r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\%$) is consistent with standards for individual-difference predictors in behavioral research \citep{cohen-1988-power}, where such magnitudes are considered ``small but real'' effects that compound at the aggregate level \citep{lakens-2013-effect-size}.
13	
14	\paragraph{Embedding anisotropy.}
15	Our centrality confound (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}) connects to representation degeneration: embeddings collapse into narrow cones \cite{gao-repr-degen-2019,ethayarajh-contextual-2019}, but local clusters can be isotropic \cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}, explaining why MAVEN's single-ontology types conflate confusability with centrality.
16	
17	
```
