    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Training-free LLM IE.}\nTraining-free LLM IE spans instruction tuning \\cite{instructuie-2023}, code-style schema encoding \\cite{knowcoder-2024,codeie-2023}, annotation-guided extraction \\cite{gollie-2024,adelie-2024}, schema-driven decoding \\cite{slot-2025,oneke-2025}, and constrained JSON decoding \\cite{json-schema-bench-2025}. For NER, methods include zero-shot decomposition \\cite{xie-zeroshot-ner-2023,promptner-2023}, distillation \\cite{uniner-2024}, and bidirectional encoders \\cite{gliner-2024,gliner2-2025}. For RE, recent work examines zero-shot relation triplet extraction via prompting \\cite{relationprompt-2022} and revisits relation extraction in the LLM era \\cite{wadhwa-re-llm-2023}. Recent work also explores LLM-based data annotation for NER \\cite{bogdanov2024nuner,zhang2023llmaaa} and self-improving zero-shot extraction \\cite{xie2024selfimproving}. These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema complexity in IE.}\nThe most directly related work is LLM-PEE \\cite{llm-pee-2025}, which partitions large event schemas by type embedding similarity. Our work differs in three ways: (1)~we conduct controlled analysis to understand \\emph{why} extraction degrades rather than developing an engineering solution; (2)~our BCS-controlled experiments isolate count from confusability, which LLM-PEE conflates; (3)~our SDGD experiments reveal that merge amplification (not partitioning quality) is the bottleneck in decompose-then-merge pipelines. Specifically, LLM-PEE employs union merge across sub-schema extractions, which our \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd} analysis shows causes precision to collapse from 0.56 to 0.21 in NER as false positives accumulate across passes; RE avoids this because closed-set classification does not require span reconciliation, explaining why LLM-PEE's decomposition succeeds for relation/event tasks but faces unresolved merge challenges on span-level extraction. Other approaches address schema complexity via hard negative mining \\cite{iepile-2024}, schema definition optimization \\cite{parse-2025}, multi-agent debate \\cite{crossagentie-2025,cmas-ner-2025}, schema-aware decomposition for event extraction \\cite{dee-2024}, and active prompting \\cite{reflect-learn-2026}; all treat it as an engineering problem rather than isolating the underlying degradation factors. Concurrently, \\citet{haystack-to-needle-2025} reduces label spaces via iterative LLM filtering, an engineering complement to our analytical approach. Our framework explains \\emph{why} such reduction works: it implicitly lowers batch confusability.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema complexity in dialogue and IE.} The Schema-Guided Dialogue dataset~\\cite{rastogi2020towards} and SGD-X~\\cite{lee2022sgd} document performance degradation with schema linguistic variation. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observe count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT IE. \\citet{volkanovska2025ner} categorize LLM NER errors including type confusion. The relationship between label similarity and classification confusion has precedent in traditional ML: hierarchical classification suffers sibling confusion proportional to inter-class proximity \\citep{deng-2010-imagenet}, label confusion matrices improve text classification \\citep{guo-2021-label-confusion}, and classification confusion captures semantic similarity \\citep{zhou-2025-rethinking}. Hierarchical label sets reduce class confusion in zero-shot image classification \\citep{novack-2023-chils}; enriched type descriptions improve zero-shot NER \\citep{cocchieri-2025-zeroner}, both orthogonal to our confusability-aware batching.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS novelty.} SCS shares its formal definition (pairwise cosine similarity of type name embeddings) with the partitioning criterion used in LLM-PEE~\\cite{llm-pee-2025} for schema decomposition. Our contribution is not the metric itself but the controlled experimental framework that isolates confusability's predictive relationship with extraction degradation, distinguishing it from the engineering use in prior work. The modest pairwise effect size ($r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$) is consistent with standards for individual-difference predictors in behavioral research \\citep{cohen-1988-power}, where such magnitudes are considered ``small but real'' effects that compound at the aggregate level \\citep{lakens-2013-effect-size}.\n\n\\paragraph{Embedding anisotropy.}\nOur centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}) connects to representation degeneration: embeddings collapse into narrow cones \\cite{gao-repr-degen-2019,ethayarajh-contextual-2019}, but local clusters can be isotropic \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}, explaining why MAVEN's single-ontology types conflate confusability with centrality.\n\n",

For RE, recent work examines zero-shot relation triplet extraction via prompting \\cite{relationprompt-2022} and revisits relation extraction in the LLM era \\cite{wadhwa-re-llm-2023}.     "originalFile": "\\section{Schema Decomposition: Failure, Diagnosis, and Mitigation}\n\\label{sec:sdgd}\n\nA natural intervention against confusability-related degradation is to partition the schema so confusable types never co-occur. We test this with \\textbf{Schema-DAG-Guided Decomposition} (SDGD), which applies greedy graph coloring on the SCS graph to assign types to low-confusability batches.\n\n\\paragraph{NER: merge amplification.}\nAll decomposition modes fall below the single-pass NER baseline (F1\\,=\\,0.445): SDGD obtains 0.251, random batching 0.320, per-type 0.292 (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}). The core failure is \\emph{merge amplification}: presenting text to the model $k$ times with different type subsets causes precision to collapse (0.563$\\to$0.210--0.249) as false positives scale with $k$, while true positives remain bounded. Confidence gating does not recover net F1. RE avoids this failure because closed-set classification requires no span reconciliation across passes.\n\n\\paragraph{RE: oracle routing.}\nOracle routing achieves 0.802 accuracy versus 0.504 single-pass on FewRel 80-relation RE (+29.8\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}), confirming that type separation recovers performance absent merge amplification. Confusability-aware grouping yields only +1.4\\,pp over random ($p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.); all practical routing strategies fall below the single-pass baseline because constrained decoding forces confident predictions in every sub-schema (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_routing}).\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}\nThree avenues may address these bottlenecks: (1)~coarse-to-fine pipelines that screen with the full schema then refine with targeted sub-schemas; (2)~retrieval-based routing using document retrieval rather than LLM confidence for sub-schema assignment; (3)~calibrated generation to recover confidence signals eliminated by constrained decoding.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{Mean BCS} \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (80 types) & 0.504 & 0.557 \\\\\nRandom batching (15 seeds)$^\\dagger$ & 0.788\\,$\\pm$\\,0.018 & 0.559 \\\\\nConfus.-aware (graph coloring) & \\textbf{0.802} & 0.552 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Batch composition on FewRel 80-relation RE with oracle routing. $^\\dagger$Mean $\\pm$ SD across 15 random seeds.}\n\\label{tab:batch_composition}\n\\end{table}\n",

85	Error taxonomy decomposition (Table~\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\text{--}27.5\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.
86	
87	These results establish two dimensions: \textbf{Dimension~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \textbf{Dimension~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), consistent with bounded association in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact. The full-range regression ($n = 69$, five count levels) serves as the primary separability test; the $2 \times 2$ factorial ($n = 54$) as a sensitivity check. Two One-Sided Tests (TOST) equivalence testing \citep{lakens-2017-equivalence} with a $\pm 3$\,pp margin ($p_{\text{TOST}} = 0.003$) confirms bounded association; sensitivity analysis at $\pm 2$\,pp is reported in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}.
88	
89	\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}
```

80	\label{sec:model}
81	
82	We use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \cite{vllm-2023} on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPU (96\,GB) with greedy decoding (temperature${}\,=\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \cite{llama3-2024}. All experiments use single-seed evaluation with greedy decoding, which substantially reduces model-level randomness (residual SD = 0.88\,pp at count 80 under greedy decoding, likely attributable to FP8 quantization non-determinism); bootstrap CIs capture the remaining sampling variance from batch composition. Pair-level CIs use percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$); batch-level CIs use $B{=}1{,}000$.
83

298	\bottomrule
299	\end{tabular}
300	\caption{BCS--accuracy Pearson correlation across four prompt variants. At count 32, all four variants show negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r \leq -0.47$), with two reaching $p < 0.05$. At count 8, the narrower BCS range reduces statistical power.}
301	\label{tab:prompt_variants}
302	\end{table}
303	
304	At count 32, all four variants produce negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r$ ranges from $-$0.47 to $-$0.77), with V2 ($p = 0.043$) and Minimal ($p = 0.009$) reaching significance and Chain-of-thought approaching it ($p = 0.088$). At count 8, correlations are weaker and nonsignificant across all variants, consistent with the narrower BCS range at lower schema complexity reducing the detectable effect size. Rather than applying Bonferroni correction, which assumes independent hypotheses inappropriate for correlated prompt variants testing the same underlying signal, we note directional consistency: 7 of 8 conditions show negative BCS--accuracy slopes, consistent with the primary finding \citep{rothman-1990}. These results confirm that the confusability effect arises from schema composition, not prompt-specific phrasing.
305	
306	\section{BCS Aggregation Sensitivity}
307	\label{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}
